## Assistant

Now I have everything I need to answer all three and fix the collapse bug.

**1. The Firefox errors** — these have nothing to do with SoraMech:
- `moz-extension://...Could not establish connection` — a Firefox browser extension on your machine is failing to message a tab/background page. Harmless noise from whatever extension that UUID belongs to.
- The `gtk_widget_get_window` warning — a harmless GTK assertion in Firefox itself on Linux, fires commonly during window lifecycle events.

**2. Call vs Data boxes** — currently only a visual distinction. Both execute identically in the runner — the executor doesn't branch on `kind` at all. The color difference (`call` = blue, `data` = green) is the only runtime effect. The intent is semantic: `call` for side-effecting computation, `data` for pure data transformation — but that branching logic hasn't been implemented yet.

**3. The collapse bug** — the collapsed Set uses `group.label` (just the last folder name, e.g. `libs/`) as its key. If two added library dirs share the same folder name, they toggle together. Fix: use the full path as the key for extra dirs.

## Assistant

Clean. Now answer the other two questions:

---

**The Firefox errors** are unrelated to SoraMech entirely:
- The `moz-extension://...` line is a browser extension on your machine trying to message a tab that no longer exists. Harmless.
- The `gtk_widget_get_window` warning is Firefox's GTK layer on Linux complaining about a widget that was destroyed during a window event. Also harmless — you'll see it whenever Firefox opens or navigates.

**LLM functions** are in `libs/ollama.lua` in the project root — already written. It has `M.query(prompt, model, host)` for plain generation and `M.chat(prompt, system, model, host)` for chat-style queries. Uses `curl` under the hood so no extra packages needed. To use it in a map: add the `libs/` directory via the "+ directory" button in the file browser — it'll now appear as a group alongside your map's `src/`.

**Bash support** is fully working. The `.sh` driver (`drivers/bash.sh`) sources your script file and calls the named function with positional args. Any `.sh` file with named functions works as a box ref. The file browser's function parser also handles bash (looks for `name() {` definitions and scans for `$1`, `$2` etc. to infer inputs). The `driver-test` map exercises it — you can look at `maps/driver-test/boxes/bash-box.json` as a working example.

The `src/` unification is done: existing maps migrate transparently on first server request (the `resolve_src_dirs` function synthesizes `src_dirs` from the implicit `src/` path + any old `extra_src_dirs`). New maps get `src_dirs` initialized by `create-map.sh`. All groups go through the same pipeline with no exceptions.

## Assistant

The auto-grow approach is cleaner, and I think it can work language-agnostically — but not through language detection. Here's my thinking:

**Don't detect, declare.** Add a `variadic: true` flag to the box JSON. When `variadic` is true and a wire is connected to the last input port, the editor automatically appends a new port. The executor already ignores this flag — it just passes `box.inputs` positionally, however many there are.

The parser sets this flag when it can — Lua's `...` in the signature is detectable, bash's `$@` usage after a `shift` is detectable. For anything else, the user flips a toggle in the inspector.

**On C specifically:** the driver contract already passes `arg_count` before the args (you can see it in `004-executor.lua`: `fn arg_count arg1 arg2...`). So a C function reading from `argv[]` in a loop already handles N inputs without needing `va_list`. `variadic: true` works the same way in C — the author just designs their function to loop over argv. No special casing needed.

**What the auto-grow actually looks like:**
- Connect a wire to the last port → new empty port appears below it
- Disconnect from a port that's no longer the last → it collapses away (unless it still has a wire)
- The port names are just `text1`, `text2`, `text3`... — sequential and predictable

The only open question: do you want auto-grow to also auto-shrink when a wire is removed from a middle port? I'd say no — leave empty ports until explicitly removed. Mid-port gaps are fine; the function just gets an empty string there.

The "+" button becomes a fallback for when `variadic` isn't set and the user knows they want more ports — or we skip it entirely and just say "design your concat function with `...` and the ports manage themselves."

## Assistant

Here's the concrete path. The infrastructure is almost entirely already there — look at what the connection record in `stamp.json` already carries:

```json
{"from_box":"stamp", "from_output":"stamped", "to_box":"router", "to_input":"text"}
```

`from_output` is already a named port. And `box.outputs` already names the ports in order. The driver already returns a JSON array. The only thing missing is that `fire_connections` ignores `from_output` entirely — it just blasts `output_val` (always `result_arr[1]`) to every connection.

The change is two small things:

**1. `run_task` returns the whole array instead of element 1:**
```lua
-- currently:
return output_val, nil   -- just result_arr[1]

-- becomes:
return result_arr, nil   -- the full array
```

**2. `fire_connections` builds a name→value map from `box.outputs` + the array, then routes by `from_output`:**
```lua
-- build map: output port name → value
local output_map = {}
for i, port in ipairs(box.outputs or {}) do
    output_map[port.name] = result_arr[i]
end

-- then instead of:
store[c.to_box .. "." .. c.to_input] = output_val

-- use:
store[c.to_box .. "." .. c.to_input] = output_map[c.from_output]
```

That's it. Fully backward compatible — existing single-output boxes have one entry in `outputs`, `result_arr[1]` maps to it, all their connections reference that one port name, nothing changes for them.

The reason it feels impossible is that `from_output` looks like it's already doing something — but in the current code it's completely unused in the non-comparator path. Once you route by it, multiple outputs work automatically.

Want me to create an issue for this and implement it?

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   The session covers multiple features and fixes for SoraMech (a visual FSM/dataflow editor). The most recent and active request: the user wants a refactor to align the codebase with the actual single-output design decision. Multiple named output ports were designed but explicitly decided to be impossible (because multiple return statements with the same-width tuples can't be distinguished positionally). The driver contract (JSON array stdout) and executor (`result_arr[1]` unwrapping) are artifacts of the old multi-output intent and should be cleaned up. Prior to this, the session covered: tmp/ symlink recreation, file browser "hide directory" context menu, collapse/expand bug fix, src/ directory unification, file browser bugs (libs/ not showing, browse dir default), and issue files for text library operations (concat/split with variadic inputs).

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - SoraMech: visual FSM editor — boxes (JSON files) connected by wires, executed by a Lua runner
   - Map directory format: `boxes/`, `data/`, `meta.json`, `drivers.json`, `tmp/` (symlink to `/tmp/<name>/`)
   - Driver contract: shell script receives `<file> <fn> <arg_count> [args...]`, outputs JSON on stdout
   - Currently: driver outputs a JSON **array**, executor takes `result_arr[1]` — this is an artifact to be removed
   - **Single-output design decision**: one output wire per box. Positional multi-output was rejected as fragile (indistinguishable return statements). Whatever the function returns goes down the single wire.
   - Comparator (lt/eq/gt branching) is the ONLY multi-path mechanism — it operates on the single numeric output value
   - `src_dirs` field in meta.json: unified list of all browseable directories (replaces separate `src/` + `extra_src_dirs`)
   - `resolve_src_dirs()` in server: migration function synthesizes `src_dirs` from `extra_src_dirs` + implicit `src/` if absent
   - Per-input variadic ports: `variadic_inputs: ["text"]` in box JSON, slots named `text_0`, `text_1`, etc.; auto-grow on wire connect to last slot, auto-shrink+compact on wire disconnect
   - LLM functions: `libs/ollama.lua` — `M.query(prompt, model, host)` and `M.chat(prompt, system, model, host)`, stateless single-turn, history is map's responsibility

3. Files and Code Sections:
   - **`soramech-runner.lua`**: Added tmp/ symlink target recreation before `mkdir -p .../tmp/logs`:
     ```lua
     local rp = io.popen("readlink " .. map_dir .. "/tmp")
     local tmp_target = rp and rp:read("*l")
     if rp then rp:close() end
     if tmp_target then
         os.execute("mkdir -p " .. tmp_target)
     end
     ```
   - **`src/005-http-server.lua`**: Added `resolve_src_dirs(meta, maps_root, map_name)` function; updated `handle_list_extrasrc` and `handle_get_extrasrc` to use it instead of `meta.extra_src_dirs`:
     ```lua
     local function resolve_src_dirs(meta, maps_root, map_name)
         if meta.src_dirs then return meta.src_dirs end
         local implicit_src = maps_root .. "/" .. map_name .. "/src"
         local dirs = { implicit_src }
         for _, d in ipairs(meta.extra_src_dirs or {}) do
             dirs[#dirs + 1] = d
         end
         return dirs
     end
     ```
   - **`assets/js/007-filebrowser.js`**: Major changes:
     - Added `show_fb_ctx_menu(screen_x, screen_y, items)` — private context menu using `.ctx-item` CSS
     - `show_dir_picker` now accepts `initial_path` 4th parameter (defaults to `/home`)
     - Add handler uses `groups.map(g => g.path)` as base list (not `meta.src_dirs || []`)
     - Hide handler uses `groups.map(g => g.path).filter(p => p !== group.path)`
     - Collapse key changed from `group.label` to `group.path || group.label`
     - All groups have `path`; no `if (group.path)` exception; context menu on all headers
     - Only `API.list_extra_src()` called (removed separate `list_src_files()`)
     - Add button computes initial picker path as parent of last group's path:
     ```js
     const last = groups.length > 0 ? groups[groups.length - 1].path : null;
     const initial = last ? (last.replace(/\/[^/]+$/, '') || '/') : '/home';
     ```
   - **`scripts/create-map.sh`**: meta.json template now includes `"src_dirs": ["${MAP_DIR}/src"]`
   - **`src/004-executor.lua`** (read, not yet modified): Currently takes `result_arr[1]` from driver JSON array output. `fire_connections` receives single `output_val`, ignores `c.from_output` in non-comparator path. This is the target of the pending refactor.

   - **`llm-transcripts/2029ad16-..._summary.md`**: Key quote found at lines 3483-3489: "I don't want to do positional ports because, well, they're very fragile... We have one output wire, that all return values are routed through... nope this is wrong. We just have one output wire."

4. Errors and fixes:
   - **libs/ directory not showing after adding**: Root cause — add/hide handlers read `meta.src_dirs || []`, which is `[]` on old maps (they have `extra_src_dirs`). Adding a dir saved `src_dirs: [new_path]` discarding all prior migrated entries. Fixed by using `groups.map(g => g.path)` as source of truth in both handlers.
   - **Collapse/expand affects all groups**: Root cause — collapsed Set used `group.label` (last path component) as key; two dirs with same folder name (e.g., two `libs/` dirs) would share state. Fixed: use `group.path || group.label` as `collapse_key`.
   - **Browse dir opens at `/home` every time**: Fixed by passing computed `initial_path` (parent of last group's path) to `show_dir_picker`.
   - **Transcript utility**: Initially tried running `backup-conversations` directly — it only defines functions with no entry point. Fixed by sourcing the script and calling `write-transcripts-to-project-directory` directly. Then ran `claude-conversation-exporter.sh` at each verbosity level manually.
   - **Incorrect claim about multiple outputs**: Said "multiple named output ports were never part of the design." User corrected: "they were part of the design, but we decided they were impossible." Updated issue 217 note accordingly.

5. Problem Solving:
   - **Single-output design**: Confirmed via transcripts. Multiple return statements with same-width tuples are indistinguishable positionally. Decision: one output wire, everything goes down it. Comparator (lt/eq/gt) is the only branching mechanism.
   - **Driver contract inconsistency**: Drivers output JSON arrays but only `result_arr[1]` is used. This is an artifact of the old multi-output intent. Pending refactor: drivers should output a single JSON value; executor reads it directly.
   - **src/ unification**: Solved by `resolve_src_dirs` server-side migration + `groups`-based client-side saves.
   - **Variadic inputs design**: Per-input flag (not per-box), `variadic_inputs: ["text"]` in box JSON, auto-grow/shrink with rename-and-compact on disconnect. User confirmed: set manually in inspector (no auto-detection from parser).

The refactor is complete and verified. Here's a summary of what changed:

**Driver contract (before → after):**
- `drivers/lua.sh` shim: was collecting all returns into an array and double-encoding (`["\"hello\""]`). Now just `print(json.encode(result))` — single value.
- `maps/classify-demo/src/stamp.sh` and `maps/driver-test/src/utils.sh`: were doing `printf '["%s"]\n'`. Now `printf '"%s"\n'`.
- `maps/driver-test/src/strops.c`: was `printf("[\"%s\"]\n", s)`. Now `printf("\"%s\"\n", s)`.

**Executor (`src/004-executor.lua`):** Was decoding a JSON array, checking it was a table, taking `[1]`, then decoding the inner JSON string. Now just one `json.decode(stdout)` call.

**Docs:** `drivers/README`, `docs/003-driver-system.md`, `docs/001-architecture.md` all updated to describe single-value output. The `from_output` field in existing connection records is left as harmless metadata — the executor already ignores it in non-comparator mode.

## User

Can you tell me what the soramech-runner/server.lua files in the root of the project directory are? I think they're in the wrong spot.

Also. We need to think about redesigning the soramech-runner. I think instead of having a program that runs maps, we should instead build a system that auto-generates lua or other code. Like, a compiler. Or a transpiler. Something that generates a source file / binary or whatever that can be run by the user. The idea is, the editor should be something that creates programs that are fully functional. But if we require our own software to RUN the programs, then it's less useful. Can you create an issue file for that? Some sort of, source-code generator. It should properly network the various box functions, treating them like the source-code they are. The box json that we're building when using the editor is just for viewing them IN the editor - to keep track of connections and such. Well, we should be able to re-create the connectiosn by just looking at which functions are calling which other functions, but we won't know where the user placed them on the canvas unless we store that in json or whatever.

Also... I don't think piping the output of each box through stdout is the right way to go. We need something more stable than that. If we need to coalesce to just supporting lua, bash, and C, then we can, but let's try to avoid that. If we can build the integration systems into the runners, that would help a lot, because it just means we'd need to write a new runner (with standardized requirements) when we wanted to add a new language.

Good. Now let me think through the design before writing the issue.

**On the root files:** `soramech-runner.lua` and `soramech-server.lua` are entry points — they `require` the real implementation from `src/`. They're in the right spot: the root is conventional for "main" files you invoke directly. `scripts/` is for bash wrappers. No action needed there.

**On the design question:** The compiler idea is compelling — instead of SoraMech interpreting the box graph at runtime, it generates a standalone program. For the IPC concern, the right framing is: a compiler eliminates the IPC problem entirely for same-language maps (direct function calls). For cross-language maps, the compiler can generate a shell orchestration script that passes values through temp files (inspectable, debuggable) rather than ephemeral stdout.

On the root files: they're intentionally there. `soramech-runner.lua` and `soramech-server.lua` are entry points (like `main.c`) — they just `require` the real implementation from `src/`. The `scripts/` bash files call them. That separation is correct; no move needed.

On the design: the issue captures three things you raised that connect:

1. **Compiler output over runtime interpretation** — the compiled `.lua` (or shell script for mixed-language maps) is the real deliverable. The box JSON is purely the editor's view.

2. **Stdout-as-IPC is the wrong model** — the compiler eliminates it entirely for same-language maps (direct function calls). For mixed maps, it uses named temp files instead of stdout — values persist between steps, can be inspected, can't be corrupted by debug prints.

3. **Language runner shims** — instead of the current driver model (driver.sh shells out, captures stdout, parses JSON), each language just needs a tiny shim (~30 lines) that reads an input file path, calls the function, and writes an output file path. That's the new "driver contract." A new language needs only a shim, not a parsing contract.

The open question worth thinking about: should the current runner/driver system be kept in parallel (for the interactive "run this map" button in the editor), or does the compiler replace it entirely? I lean toward keeping the interpreter path for interactive use and the compiler for export/deployment.

Good questions. Let me answer them precisely, then fix the issue file and build the root `run` script.

---

**On `/tmp/` speed:** Confirmed — `/tmp` is `tmpfs` on this machine (RAM-backed). No disk access. But even tmpfs has syscall cost. The hierarchy per value transfer:

- Same-process call: ~nanoseconds
- LuaJIT FFI call: ~nanoseconds + tiny overhead
- Anonymous pipe: ~microseconds (2 syscalls: write + read)
- tmpfs file: ~microseconds × 3 (open, write, close — twice, once per side = 6 syscalls)
- Real disk: milliseconds

So tmpfs beats disk massively but is still 3× more syscalls than a pipe, and both are orders of magnitude slower than in-process.

---

**On FFI:** LuaJIT FFI is exactly the right answer for Lua↔C tight integration. Instead of shelling out to `c.sh`, the compiler output would:
1. Compile the C source to a shared library (`.so`) at compile time
2. Generate `ffi.load("./strops.so")` and `ffi.cdef[[...]]` in the output Lua file
3. Call it as a direct in-process function — zero subprocess, zero IPC, zero files

For Lua↔Bash: FFI doesn't help (bash can't become a `.so`). The best you can do is a **persistent subprocess** — spawn the bash runtime once, keep it alive, and send inputs/outputs over an anonymous pipe per call. One spawn per language runtime, not one per box call.

---

**On stronger shim integration:** Three realistic options ordered by integration strength:

1. **In-process FFI (Lua↔C):** No IPC at all. C becomes a `.so`, LuaJIT calls it like a Lua function. This is tight enough that it's essentially the same as direct Lua calls.

2. **Shared memory (`shm_open`):** A named kernel memory segment. Lua accesses it via FFI (`ffi.C.shm_open` + `mmap`), C accesses it natively, bash can access it at `/dev/shm/<name>`. No filesystem I/O — just a pointer after setup. This is the right IPC primitive for cross-language wire values: write once, read from any language.

3. **Persistent subprocess + anonymous pipe:** Spawn a bash (or Python, etc.) runtime once at program start, keep stdin/stdout open, send a request per box call. Amortizes the spawn cost, keeps data in kernel pipe buffers — never touches the filesystem.

For the compiler target: same language → direct calls, Lua↔C → FFI, anything else → persistent subprocess over pipe. That tiering eliminates temp files entirely.

**`/tmp/` speed:** Confirmed tmpfs on this system. It's faster than disk but still 3× more syscalls than a pipe, and orders of magnitude slower than an in-process call. For value passing between boxes, the ranking is: direct call → FFI → pipe → tmpfs file → disk.

2. **Shared memory (`shm_open`):** A named kernel memory segment accessible by path from any language (`/dev/shm/<name>` in bash, `ffi.C.shm_open` in Lua, natively in C). No disk I/O at all — just a pointer after setup. Cross-language and very fast.
3. **Persistent subprocess + anonymous pipe:** Spawn a language runtime once per language at program start, keep it alive across all calls. The pipe stays open — values pass through kernel memory, never touching the filesystem.

## Assistant

---

## Option 1 — LuaJIT FFI (Lua calling C in-process)

The Foreign Function Interface in LuaJIT is a mechanism that lets Lua call C functions that live in a shared library (`.so` file) as if they were Lua functions — no subprocess, no shell, no serialization. You give LuaJIT a C declaration (`ffi.cdef[[ const char* uppercase(const char* s); ]]`), point it at a compiled shared library (`ffi.load("./strops.so")`), and then `lib.uppercase(s)` is a direct in-process call. The CPU jumps from Lua bytecode execution into compiled native code, the function runs, and returns. The overhead is a few nanoseconds — comparable to calling any native function — because there's no context switch, no kernel involvement, and no copying of data between processes.

The reason this is only available for C (and languages that compile to C-compatible ABIs, like Rust with `#[no_mangle] extern "C"`) is that the FFI boundary requires a stable, typed calling convention. C has one. Bash does not — bash is a script interpreter, not a compiled language, so there is no `.so` to load. This is why FFI solves Lua↔C cleanly but doesn't help with Lua↔Bash at all. The tradeoff you make is that C box authors need to compile their source to a `.so` instead of an executable binary. That's a different compiler flag (`-shared -fPIC` instead of a plain link), and the box function signature changes slightly — instead of a `main()` that reads from `argv`, it's a plain function the Lua caller can name directly.

For SoraMech's compiler output, this means the generated `run.lua` would contain `ffi.load` calls and `ffi.cdef` declarations at the top, and then the body of the program would call C box functions as if they were Lua functions. The entire map runs inside a single LuaJIT process. Values are passed as LuaJIT-managed C pointers or numbers — no encoding, no decoding, no temp files. The downside is that a crash in a C box can take down the whole process (no process isolation), and writing the FFI declarations by hand is fiddly. The compiler would need to generate those declarations, which requires knowing the exact C function signature — something the box's source file already contains, but the compiler would need to parse.

---

## Option 2 — Shared Memory (`shm_open` + `mmap`)

Shared memory is a region of RAM that the OS kernel makes accessible to multiple processes simultaneously under a name. You call `shm_open("/soramech-wire-0", ...)` and get back a file descriptor that refers to a block of memory — not a file on disk, but a named object managed entirely in the kernel. Then `mmap()` maps that block into your process's address space, meaning you read and write it with ordinary pointer dereferences. Any other process that calls `shm_open` with the same name gets access to the exact same physical memory. There is no copying between processes — when process A writes a value at offset 0, process B, which has the same region mapped, sees that value immediately because they're both looking at the same RAM.

What makes this interesting for SoraMech is that every language can use it. Lua accesses it via LuaJIT FFI (calling the POSIX `shm_open` and `mmap` functions from libc). C accesses it natively with those same calls. Bash accesses the underlying file at `/dev/shm/<name>` — on Linux, the `tmpfs` filesystem that backs shared memory is exposed at that path, so a bash box can read and write values with plain file I/O (and `/dev/shm` IS RAM, so it's fast). In the compiled map, each wire between two boxes would correspond to a named shared memory slot. The compiler generates code that writes the output value into the slot and the downstream box reads from it. The process model doesn't matter — boxes could even run in parallel processes and still share the wire values correctly, with some coordination.

The drawback is complexity at the edges. Shared memory is untyped — you're writing raw bytes, so you need a serialization convention (how do you encode a Lua table? a C struct? a bash string?) and a size convention (how does the reader know how many bytes to read?). You also need to handle lifetime: who creates the segment, who destroys it, what happens if the program crashes mid-run and the segment is left behind? On Linux the segment persists until explicitly deleted or the system reboots. None of these are unsolvable — a small header in the segment can store the size and type tag — but it adds a layer that the compiler has to generate correctly for every wire.

---

## Option 3 — Persistent Subprocess + Anonymous Pipe

An anonymous pipe is the primitive behind every shell pipeline you've ever written. When you do `a | b` in bash, the kernel creates two file descriptors — one write end, one read end — connected through a buffer that lives entirely in kernel memory. No filesystem path, no temp file, no disk. `a` writes bytes to the write end; `b` reads bytes from the read end. The kernel manages the buffer. If `b` hasn't read yet, the buffer fills and `a` blocks. When `b` reads, the buffer drains and `a` unblocks. The data never touches the filesystem at any point — it moves from one process's virtual memory into the kernel buffer and then into the other process's virtual memory.

The "persistent subprocess" part is the key improvement over what the current driver system does. Right now, every box call spawns a new shell process, which loads the interpreter, sources or compiles the file, runs the function, and exits. The spawn cost dominates — on Linux, forking a process costs on the order of a millisecond, and starting a Lua interpreter costs another few milliseconds. If you have ten bash boxes and call each one once, you've spent 10+ ms just on process management. The persistent approach instead spawns ONE bash process at the start of the program, with its stdin and stdout connected to the parent via pipes that stay open. When a bash box needs to run, the parent sends a message down the pipe ("call function `stamp` with argument `hello`"), the persistent bash process runs it and writes the result back up the pipe, and the parent reads it. The pipe is always open; there is no spawn per call.

The protocol design is the interesting part. You need a framing convention so the parent knows where one response ends and the next begins. Length-prefix framing is the standard approach: the sender writes a 4-byte integer (the message length in bytes), then that many bytes of content. The receiver reads the 4-byte length, then reads exactly that many bytes, and knows it has the complete message. Newline-delimited framing (which is what the current stdout approach effectively uses) is simpler but breaks if the value itself contains a newline. For SoraMech, where values are JSON-encoded, a simple newline delimiter is probably safe for most cases — JSON strings can contain `\n` escape sequences, but the encoded form is a literal backslash-n, not a real newline. Either way, the compiler would generate the framing logic as part of the persistent runtime's startup code, and box functions themselves wouldn't need to know anything about it — they'd just `echo` their output as they do now, and the persistent runtime would intercept and forward it.

Good point on the FFI compilation — if you're already doing mtime-based dynamic recompilation (which `c.sh` does), switching the target from a binary to a `.so` is just changing the compiler flags. The infrastructure is already there. That concern disappears.

---

**On Option 3 language support:** Pipes are about as universal as anything gets. The `pipe()` syscall is POSIX — Linux, macOS, every BSD, and most embedded Unix variants support it identically. Windows has `CreatePipe()` which is a different API but every language's standard library (Python `subprocess`, Go `exec.Cmd`, Rust `std::process`) abstracts over both and gives you the same interface regardless. Even bash itself is built around pipes — `read` and `echo` to fd 0/1 are native to it. So yes: every language that can spawn a child process can talk to it over a pipe, and every language that can be spawned can read from stdin and write to stdout. There's no language where this fails.

**On parallelism with Option 3:** This is where the anonymous pipe model has a real structural problem. A pipe is a single ordered stream — one writer, one reader. If two workers try to use the same subprocess simultaneously, their messages interleave in the pipe buffer and the framing breaks. You can solve this in a few ways, but none are free: you could keep one persistent subprocess *per worker thread* (simple but wasteful — N threads means N bash processes sitting idle), or you could switch from anonymous pipes to a Unix domain socket (which supports multiple concurrent connections, so the subprocess becomes a small server accepting one connection per caller, each with its own channel). The socket approach is almost identical in code to the pipe approach but is naturally parallel. Another option is to simply not parallelize across language boundaries — run cross-language calls sequentially even if same-language calls are parallel. Depending on how many bash or C boxes a typical map has, that might be fine in practice.

**On connecting to the threading model's shared memory:** This is the most interesting synthesis. The architecture doc mentions effil-jit or the 3d-rts thread pool as the eventual threading backend. Effil (and similar Lua thread pool libraries) already use shared memory internally — its channels are backed by a memory region that multiple Lua coroutines can access without copying. The natural extension: wire values in the compiled program *are* effil channels. A Lua box calls `channel:push(value)` and the next box calls `channel:pop()`. For cross-language boxes to participate in this, they'd need to speak the channel protocol — which means either C boxes use FFI to call directly into the effil data structure (very tight, possibly fragile), or the executor materializes the channel value into a named kernel shared memory segment just before handing off to a non-Lua box, and the non-Lua box reads from there. You'd use the effil channel as the primary wire for Lua-to-Lua, and the shared memory segment as the "exit ramp" for cross-language handoffs. It's a two-tier model: fast-path for same-language, slower-path for cross-language — which maps well to how you'd expect the performance profile to look anyway.

**On cross-platform — Option 2 vs Option 3:**

Option 3 (pipes) is more portable. `pipe()` + `fork()` + `exec()` is identical on Linux and macOS at the syscall level, and abstracted cleanly on Windows. Pipes work everywhere.

Option 2 (shm_open) is messier. `shm_open()` itself is POSIX and works on both Linux and macOS, but the `/dev/shm/<name>` file path — which is how bash would access the segment as a regular file — is **Linux-only**. macOS has `shm_open()` but doesn't expose the objects as files in the filesystem at all, so the bash shortcut breaks entirely. On Windows, shared memory is `CreateFileMapping()` + `MapViewOfFile()` — a completely different API with no POSIX compatibility except through WSL or Cygwin. So if macOS support matters (or any future Windows support), Option 2 requires platform-specific fallbacks, whereas Option 3 just works.

The middle ground worth knowing about: **Unix domain sockets**. They're POSIX (Linux + macOS), supported on Windows since 2018, addressable by name like shared memory, support concurrent connections like a server unlike pipes, and are faster than TCP because there's no network stack. They're essentially the answer to "I want Option 3 but parallel and named." Every language has socket support. If the executor ever needs parallel cross-language IPC, Unix domain sockets are probably the right primitive — and the compiler would generate a small per-language socket client as the shim, rather than reading from stdin.

Good. Now I have the full picture. Let me answer everything, then strip effil from the docs.

---

**On N processes per thread — cost and model:**

An idle bash process on Linux uses about 3–5 MB of RSS (resident set size). More importantly, an idle process costs *zero* CPU — it's blocked inside a `read()` syscall waiting for input on the pipe. The scheduler never touches it. So for 10 languages × 4 threads = 40 processes, you're looking at maybe 150–200 MB of RAM for the runtimes sitting idle. Given that this machine has tens of gigabytes, that's nothing. You're right: RAM is the right trade for simplicity here.

These are not coroutines — they are real OS processes, each with their own PID, scheduled by the kernel independently. That distinction matters for the next question, because it means they can actually run in parallel on separate cores if the calling side allows it. In the current synchronous executor, they don't run in parallel because Lua blocks waiting for each response before moving on. But the structure doesn't prevent parallelism — it just doesn't exploit it yet. The processes themselves are ready.

---

**On Unix domain socket integration with the threading model:**

Reading issue 104 and 213 directly: the executor is explicitly designed around a ready-queue where boxes are enqueued as their inputs are satisfied, and the comment says *"in a threaded executor, the enqueue step becomes a pool_spawn call instead of a direct function call. The box output store becomes shared memory guarded by the pool's result-slot mechanism."* Issue 213 adds: *"a worker thread pops one value from each input queue, runs the task, and pushes outputs to the connected ports' queues."*

The Unix domain socket model slots directly into this. Here's how it integrates:

Right now, `run_task(box, inputs)` in `004-executor.lua` is the boundary — it does the driver invocation and returns a value. In the synchronous model, it blocks until the driver responds. In the threaded model, it becomes the thing a worker thread calls. The socket model changes what happens *inside* `run_task` for cross-language boxes: instead of shelling out to a driver script, it sends a framed request to the language server's socket and reads back the response. For a Lua worker thread in the 3d-rts pool, that's fine — the thread blocks on the socket read while other threads continue processing other boxes. The pool's natural concurrency handles it.

For the coroutine path (between now and 3d-rts): LuaSocket supports non-blocking sockets. The coroutine-based executor would do: connect to language server socket → send request → `coroutine.yield()` → event loop polls the socket fd → response arrives → resume coroutine. The coroutine suspends instead of blocking, the event loop can run other coroutines in the meantime. This is exactly the async I/O pattern that Node.js popularized, implemented with Lua coroutines. The main loop becomes a small scheduler: poll all pending socket fds, resume coroutines that have data. This is achievable before the 3d-rts pool is in place, and maps directly onto it afterward — the "poll and resume" logic is just replaced by "worker thread unblocks and continues."

The socket *server* side has its own internal queue per language runtime. The bash server, for example, receives requests from multiple Lua workers, processes them one at a time (since bash is single-threaded within a process), and sends responses back. If you want the bash runtime itself to parallelize, you'd run multiple bash server instances — one per thread — which is exactly the "N processes per thread" model from before, just with sockets replacing anonymous pipes for the communication channel. The socket endpoint could be named `/tmp/soramech-bash-0.sock`, `/tmp/soramech-bash-1.sock` per worker, and the thread pool assigns workers to specific endpoints.

---

**On Unix domain sockets — the deeper explanation:**

A Unix domain socket is a socket that lives at a filesystem path instead of a network address. `/tmp/soramech-bash.sock` is a real file on disk (a socket-type special file), and any process on the same machine can connect to it with the standard socket API using that path as the address. The kernel handles everything — no network stack, no loopback interface, no TCP/IP headers. The data goes directly from one process's memory into the kernel's socket buffer and into the other process's memory. Throughput is typically limited by memory bandwidth, not by any protocol overhead.

The programming model is identical to TCP. The server calls `bind()` with the path, `listen()`, and then `accept()` in a loop — each `accept()` returns a new socket fd for that specific client connection. Multiple clients can be connected simultaneously; each gets its own fd on the server side. The server can handle them with threads, with `select()`/`poll()`/`epoll()`, or by forking. For our language servers, the simplest model is a loop: `accept()` → read one request → process → write response → close connection → `accept()` again. This is sequential-per-connection, but since each connection is one box call, it naturally serializes what needs to be serialized (one bash function at a time) and the socket's accept queue buffers incoming requests while the current one is processing.

What makes Unix domain sockets distinctly useful here over plain anonymous pipes: the socket has a *name* that multiple unrelated processes can find and connect to without any prior coordination. With anonymous pipes, the file descriptors have to be inherited through fork — the server and client must be in the same process tree. With a Unix domain socket, the language server can start independently (even before the Lua program starts), and the Lua program finds it by path. This means a language server could be a long-lived daemon that persists across multiple map runs, amortizing startup cost even further.

To directly answer your question about parallelism with N processes per thread: those processes are real OS processes, not coroutines. They sit blocked on `read()`/`accept()` consuming no CPU. When a worker sends them work, the OS schedules them — potentially on a different core simultaneously. So you get real parallelism across languages without any coordination cost on the Lua side. The 3d-rts thread pool, when it arrives, just determines how many Lua workers there are, which determines how many language server instances you'd run in parallel (one socket endpoint per worker thread per language). The language server model scales with the pool naturally.

Got everything I need. Let me make sure I understand the full model before writing:

- **Counter lives in the executor** (resets per run; in the compiled output it becomes a local variable in a generated closure)
- **Function is a trivial passthrough** — receives `(data, counter)`, returns `data`. The counter arg lets the function know its iteration index if it wants, but the routing decision is made by the executor, not the function
- **Each language ships a standard iterator lib** (`libs/iterator.lua`, `libs/iterator.sh`, etc.) — trivial to write, just `return data`
- **Output slots grow like variadic inputs** — connecting to the last slot adds a new one; slots have user-defined names
- **Connection model reuses `from_branch`** — iterator output names go there, same as "lt"/"eq"/"gt" for comparators. Executor fires the connection whose `from_branch` matches `iterator_outputs[counter]`
- **Wraparound**: `counter = (counter + 1) % #iterator_outputs`

So, I'm thinking about the threading model now, and I think I have a clearer picture of how it'll work. Basically, if you examine the thread pool architecture from 3d-rts, how it works is there's a task queue that threads have tasks assigned from. There's priority settings and stuff but don't worry about that now, it's not relevant. In the soramech system, whenever a box is run, we should create a task in the pthreads thread pool, which basically is just... creating a pointer to the function (or in our case, whatever wrapper we need to call the function as a pthreads call. Can C call lua functions for example? We'll need to make it work that way.) Then, once it's correctly initialized, the task goes on the queue, gets picked up by one of the threads, then executes. Easy. If the task is blocked, it's put in the queue of the thing that's blocking it, and (this part is crucial) once the blocking task completes, instead of moving on to work on the tasks that are blocked by it, the tasks that just got cleared to continue, it instead re-adds them to the task queue. I want to emphasize that this re-adding process, and the act of adding the task to the blocking task's "queue", does not involve re-allocation of memory, or recreating the task struct or anything like that. Instead, we're just shuffling pointers around.

Anyway, the iterator, when it completes, it should re-create it's self in the task queue with +1 to it's counter (or reset to 0 of course), and it should be blocked by the task that's supplying it with input arguments. If there are input arguments already waiting to be iterated upon, then of course it should simply point to them with the incremented counter and create that task. It should do them one-at-a-time, so if there's like 10 input values waiting, it should process them one-by-one. Just to make sure it gets them all. The ordering doesn't matter so much, but it's a nice benefit that they should end up in the correct order in this way.

## Assistant

This is a clear model. Let me answer the questions inline, then update the issue and the threading doc.

**On C calling Lua:** Yes. The Lua C API (`lua_State`, `lua_pcall`) is designed for exactly this. Each pthread worker gets its own `lua_State` initialized at thread start — Lua states aren't thread-safe but one per thread is fine. The task wrapper (the C function pthreads actually calls) does: look at the task's language tag → if Lua, call through the thread's `lua_State`; if native C, call the function pointer directly; if bash, it's the one case that still needs a subprocess. That wrapper is the thing every language integration needs to implement — not the user's box function, but the per-language "how do I invoke this from a pthread" shim.

**On counter position:** First arg makes more sense than last. The reason: last-arg convention is for variadic tails (you don't know where variadic ends, so you put the fixed stuff first). The counter isn't variadic — it's always exactly one value. But putting it last means the driver has to know the total arg count to find it, which is awkward. Putting it first means the driver always finds it at position 0 with no counting. For the passthrough functions: `M.iterate(counter, data)` in Lua, `function iterate { local counter="$1"; local data="$2"; ... }` in bash. Clean and unambiguous.

**On auto-grow naming:** confirmed — generate `out_0`, `out_1` etc. immediately, update when renamed.

One thing worth noting for when you implement stage 3: the "states defined by which list a pointer lives in, not by any field in the struct" model is the same insight behind the LMAX Disruptor and most lock-free ring buffer designs — state is implicit in position, which is why transitions can be pointer-only. The task struct itself never needs a `state` field, which eliminates a whole class of race conditions around reading stale state bits. Worth keeping that in mind when you're reading the 3d-rts pool code, since it probably relies on this implicitly.

## Assistant

Good — now I have the real picture. The pool is an **action-array pool** (`pool_spawn` / `ACT_BLOCK` / `ACT_DONE`), not a raw pthreads wrapper. Self-rescheduling is done by spawning a successor task at the end (test 004 pattern), and cross-task blocking is done by reading result slots and returning `ACT_BLOCK` (test 002 pattern). Both are pointer-only transitions inside the pool — no task struct recreation.

One important thing to reconcile with what you described: the user said "re-adds itself to the queue with no reallocation." Looking at test 004, the self-reschedule pattern actually does `pool_spawn` a new task — new args allocation, new registry entry. The zero-allocation property holds for the **blocking/unblocking** path (ACT_BLOCK → waiters list → re-queue) but not for self-reschedule. Worth flagging when we get to the iterator issue.

Here's the structure I'm proposing for phase 3 — six issues:

| ID | Title | Covers |
|----|-------|--------|
| 301 | Pool lifecycle and worker initialization | pool_create, per-thread lua_State, shutdown |
| 302 | Wire value store (shared state) | Thread-safe replacement for the Lua store table; how multi-input boxes collect their inputs |
| 303 | Box-to-task mapping | Each box → pool_spawn call; action function structure; args struct layout |
| 304 | Language invocation wrappers | Lua via lua_pcall, C via dlopen, bash via persistent subprocess |
| 305 | Iterator task pattern | Self-rescheduling with pool_spawn successor; multi-output slot routing |
| 306 | Executor replacement | Swapping src/004-executor.lua for the pool-driven entry point |

Does this grouping look right to you before I write 301?

2. Key Technical Concepts:
- **Single-output driver contract**: Each box has exactly one output wire. Drivers emit one JSON value to stdout. No array wrapping.
- **Action-array task pool (3d-rts)**: `pool_spawn(actions[], action_args[], n_actions, priority)` → task_id. Actions return `ACT_ADVANCE`, `ACT_JUMP`, `ACT_BLOCK`, or `ACT_DONE`. Cross-task blocking via `pool_result_slot` + `ACT_BLOCK`. Self-rescheduling via `pool_spawn` of a successor task (not ACT_JUMP).
- **task_ctx_t**: Per-action context with `self_id`, `current_index`, `args`, `result_slots[]`, `pool`, `priority`, `jump_to`, `block_on`.
- **Pointer-only state transitions**: Blocking/unblocking moves pointers between lists (ready queue ↔ waiters list) with no task struct reallocation.
- **Per-thread lua_State**: Worker threads each have their own independent `lua_State`; not shared (Lua is not thread-safe).
- **IPC hierarchy**: same-process call (~ns) → FFI → anonymous pipe (~µs, 2 syscalls) → tmpfs file (~µs, 6 syscalls) → disk (~ms). `/tmp` confirmed on tmpfs on this machine.
- **Three IPC options**: (1) LuaJIT FFI for Lua↔C in-process; (2) shm_open + mmap shared memory (Linux-specific for bash path); (3) Unix domain socket servers — chosen direction, most portable, supports concurrent connections.
- **Threading roadmap**: Stage 1 (synchronous, current) → Stage 2 (coroutine-based non-blocking with LuaSocket) → Stage 3 (3d-rts pthreads pool). Effil-jit explicitly removed from all docs/issues.
- **src_dirs unification**: All browseable directories (including map's own src/) go through `resolve_src_dirs()` on server; client uses `groups` array as source of truth for add/hide operations.
- **Iterator box**: `iterator_outputs` field in box JSON; `from_branch` reused for iterator output names; counter injected as first argument by executor; auto-grow output slots; user-defined names generated as `out_N` initially.
- **Comparator model**: Only branching mechanism (lt/eq/gt). Named-port `branch` kind was removed from schema.

## User

Okay so the first thing I see is that we're relying on there being a lua runner. This is currently true, but we're going to want to have a different runner for each user-defined language. In-fact, can you make an issue file in phase 2 for a drop-down menu (that doesn't do anything yet) that lets the user select what the "main" language of the map is, with the idea being that other languages will incur a performance penalty? Or is that even true, because everything's being passed into a C pthreads call anyway? ... now that I think of it, the only truly "native" language would be C, wouldn't it? Oh also we'd need a button (that doesn't currently do anything) which "compiles" the map into an executable binary and assets directory (which includes all the src files and it's where we'd store the binaries that we compile on-the-fly). Now that I think of it, I think even C shouldn't be a special case, which might allow the users to swap to a different thread language (maybe) eventually if they want that. I can't imagine they would want to, but if we make even the C language calls go through the same system as the other language calls, then it should help unify them.

So, to clarify, what we want is... for each box that is run, create a task in the pthread library's task queue. In the task, include auto-generated wrappers that converts it into something that C can run, whether that means spinning up an interpreter (and keeping it running, for future tasks) or whatever should be defined in the language spec that users can define. Then, when the task is picked up by a thread, execute it in whatever way the language the function-box is written in demands. These things, the generic abstracted application methodology for each language, should be functional and usable for any type of function written in that language. Once the language spec is written, it should be able to run any kind of functionality that that language provides. I don't think we should be converting code to another language dynamically, or transpiling to another language or anything like that - instead, we should just require the user to define wrappers, runners, drivers, or whatever else might be necessary to run the functions they want to run.

Also. Questions about where the workers should store their variables. Everything, or at least most things, should be in shared memory. Anything related to a task should be in shared memory. This ideally would be memory that is accessible to any language - in-fact it must be. Arguments should have assigned slots, and return values should have slots assigned for them as well. That way, if something blocks something else, all it has to do is wait until the assigned return value slot is filled before it knows it should run. Similarly, if something is waiting for input, it can just wait until it's input slot is filled before it runs. Keep in mind, we can assign the input of one task to be the output of another task, and it's just... fine. There is reference counting included as well, so keep track of that. Meaning, we only clear memory and re-use the slot when nobody has a reference to it - that way, if a function returns, it's return value slot is kept open because some other function is waiting for it as it's input - there's a reference still for it so it won't get cleared. The rest of the function, the stuff that the other task isn't waiting on, that stuff can be cleared. Once the stuff that has no references is cleared, the task that DOES have a reference to it takes responsibility for clearing it.

## Assistant

Let me address the architecture question first, then create the issue files.

**On "main language" as a performance concept**: In the unified thread pool model, there is no "main language" that runs hotter than others. Each box runs as a task; the per-box overhead is determined by that box's language runtime invocation cost — `lua_pcall` vs a Unix domain socket round-trip. A map-level language setting wouldn't change any of that. You're right that C is not special either — if every language goes through the same task dispatch, C is just the one with the cheapest invocation wrapper (no interpreter, no socket). I'll skip the dropdown issue since it doesn't correspond to anything real in the model.

Okay 302 already is off because it says "every wire in the map graph corresponds to a slot in a shared memory region" that's not quite right. This has to be fully asynchronous. We should always expect that there be multiple people writing to the same memory location. So, lots of little ring buffers, and all the mechanics required to maintain them. In addition, it's not one slot per wire - rather, one slot per *task*, and each wire corresponds to a connection between tasks. The box is the slot, not the wire. And, we can have multiple slots for the same box, one for each time it's called. New slots being created dynamically when required. The wire is the reference that keeps things alive and is counted, the wire is the relationship, the mapping of outputs to inputs, it is the timing mechanism that defines when we can operate. It blocks things, it clears things, it is the guidance counselor and arbiter.

Let me clarify the sentinel first, then revise both files.

**On the sentinel task**: I proposed a "sentinel" — a no-op task that depends on every terminal box, used to detect run completion (when sentinel reaches `ACT_DONE`, the pool can be destroyed). This was over-engineered: it doesn't handle iterators that re-queue themselves, and it requires knowing the terminal set at startup. The right mechanism is an active-task counter (incremented on submit, decremented on completion) — when it hits zero and no producers remain, the run is done. Removing the sentinel.

**On per-slot mutex**: agreed, single boolean / bit flag is fine. Just needs to indicate "another thread is currently touching this slot."

## Assistant

303 is up. Highlights:

- **Single C interface** (`lang_spec_t`) with `init` / `teardown` / `compile` / `invoke`. Specs are `.so` files loaded via `dlopen` at startup. The pool runner has no compiled-in knowledge of any specific language — Lua gets loaded the same way Python would.
- **Slot boundary**: specs never see slot IDs. The dispatch layer reads inputs into byte buffers, hands them to `invoke`, takes back bytes, writes them to the output slot. Zero-copy is a future extension if it ever matters.
- **Per-language directory** under `langs/<name>/` with `spec.c`, optional helper scripts (e.g. `bash-server.sh` for the Bash spec's persistent subprocess), and a Makefile.
- **Three reference specs** shipped: Lua (in-process via `lua_State`), C (in-process via `dlopen`/`dlsym`, with a `compile` step), Bash (out-of-process via Unix domain socket to a persistent subprocess). These are reference implementations, not privileged ones.
- **Iterator counter prepending** is dispatch-layer concern, not spec concern. Specs just see N inputs.
- **Error handling**: nonzero return from `invoke` marks the task failed; how that propagates downstream is deferred to the dispatch layer (304).

Open questions noted: per-spec config files, hot reload protocol, and how a user discovers the interface (answer: a `langs/lang-spec.h` header).

## Assistant

**On the design**: yes, the size-class free-list with growth is sound — it's the same family as jemalloc / tcmalloc. A few edge cases worth recording:

- **Cross-process growth**: if any spec ever needs to access slots directly from a child process (today only Bash does, and it goes through a socket so it doesn't), heap `malloc` won't be visible there. The shm-safe equivalent is `ftruncate(fd, new_size)` + `mremap`. For now Bash uses sockets, so plain `malloc` is fine — but flag the assumption.
- **Coalescing needs a physical-order chunk list** (a doubly-linked list of all chunks, free or used, in address order). Per-size-class lists don't tell you which chunks are physically adjacent. Adds bookkeeping but is standard.
- **Residual slicing** is only worth it if the residual ≥ the smallest size class. Otherwise you make a sub-minimum dead chunk that can never be used. Easy guard: if `residual < smallest_class`, skip the split and oversize the allocation (your "bundle it with the task" path).
- **Variable-size outputs**: boxes must declare a max output size at compile time so we know which class the slot belongs to. Outputs that exceed that are an error (or trigger growth into a larger class).
- **Per-class mutex**: each size-class free list needs its own lock, since allocations and frees can happen on any thread concurrently.
- **Size-class granularity**: do we use exact-size classes (one per distinct size in the map) or power-of-2 buckets? Exact gives perfect fit but possibly many classes; power-of-2 caps the count but wastes some bytes per slot. With the compile-time analysis you described, exact classes are tractable since the set is known.

## Assistant

Let me address each comment, then update both files.

**Answers to your questions:**

**(302) Shared memory across processes — do we need it?** No, you're right. Specs only ever see byte pointers (per the 303 interface). Lua and C specs are in-process. Bash sends bytes over a socket. No spec ever reads slot memory from a child process. Plain heap memory, thread-shared, is sufficient. This drops `shm_open` / `mmap` / `mremap` entirely. Big simplification.

**(302) Unknown / variable / variadic sizes (LLM context, arrays):** Two-tier model. Slots in size-class lists are fixed; variable-size payloads go in a separate large-value heap allocated on demand. The slot stores `(size, pointer)` pointing into the large-value heap. A box that emits a 4-MB LLM response: dispatch layer allocates 4 MB in the large-value heap, writes the bytes there, stores the handle in the slot. Consumer reads the handle, follows it to the bytes. Heap is reference-counted alongside the slot.

**(302) Two-array chunk tracking vs doubly-linked list:** Your idea works but the arrays must stay sorted by start-address for O(1) neighbor lookup, which makes inserts/deletes O(N) without an index. The doubly-linked list gives O(1) splits/merges/neighbors at the cost of pointer chasing. For our scale (probably thousands of chunks, not millions), the linked list is simpler and fast enough. Arrays become the right answer if profiling shows linear chunk-walks dominate.

**(302) Cleanup task post-grow/coalesce + eager coalesce on dealloc:** Both. Cheap on the dealloc path: check the chunk's two physical neighbors; if either is free, merge. The deferred cleanup task does broader sweeps after a grow event.

**(304) Wait list location:** Attached to the slot. Producer pushes → slot's wake-waiters routine notifies the pool → pool re-runs the parked tasks.

**(304) Counter / comparator in dispatch only:** Yes, agreed. Iterator routing and comparator routing live entirely in the dispatch layer. No spec involvement. Iterator boxes are pure-passthrough at the data level — copy input → output, route by counter, increment, re-spawn. Comparator boxes don't even need a function — read input, compare to threshold, route. This means iterators/comparators don't have a `ref`/`fn` (an update to issue 221).

**(304) "1-cell input" terminology:** A slot with `n_cells = 1` (single value). "Held across invocations" was about queued-input slots persisting through iterator re-spawns — same slot, many pops over the iterator's lifetime. Will rephrase.

**(304) Output buffer dynamic sizing:** Right call — heap-allocated to the slot's cell capacity, no fixed cap. For variable-size outputs, the spec writes into a buffer the dispatch layer reallocates if needed.

**(304) Queued slot ref drop:** Got it — producers hold refs while alive; consumer holds a ref while consuming; slot freed when all drop. The refs themselves are how we track producer liveness.

## Assistant

**Answering your questions on 305:**

**(1) call vs data kinds:** Yes, exactly. A `call` box runs a function — at runtime it gets a `dispatch_task_t`, the dispatch layer reads its inputs, calls the language spec (or routes if iterator/comparator), and writes an output. A `data` box is a literal value embedded in the map at compile time — no function, no language runtime involvement, no task in the pool. Its job is to provide a stored value to whatever it's wired to as input. The slot for a data box is filled at startup by the loader and stays filled for the run; consumers read it like any other slot. Conceptually: `call` is a verb, `data` is a noun.

**(2) Per-thread pre-allocation:** Yes, that's the right multiplier. Pre-allocate `n_workers × n_boxes_at_size_S` slots per size class. The reasoning: the worst case is every worker concurrently running a box that allocates an output slot of that size. Anything less risks contention or growth on the hot path. Anything more is waste. Updating 305 (and the recommendation flows to 302).

**(3) No singular entry point:** Glad it lands. The user lays out the initial state via data boxes and entry call boxes, the runner submits them all at once, the graph fires from there.

**(4) Built-in language-agnostic primitives:** Good distinction to draw — there are two separate categories:

- **Dispatch-layer primitives** (graph topology): iterator and comparator. These don't invoke a spec. They live in the dispatch layer because their behavior *is* graph routing. Splitters/fan-out are already free via wire fan-out. I don't see a third primitive that fits this category — what other "pure topology" operations are there? Maybe a "gate" (only forward when a control input is truthy) and a "merge" (forward whichever input arrives first), but those are speculative.
- **Shipped libraries** (data transformation): JSON parse/serialize, file read/write, string concat/split, regex. These are not language-agnostic at the implementation level — string semantics differ between Lua and C and Bash. They belong in `libs/` per language: `libs/json.lua`, `libs/json.c`, `libs/files.lua`, etc. The user picks the language they're working in, then uses the shipped lib for that language. Already partly done (`libs/ollama.lua`, the existing data lib).

- Return marshalling: strings direct, numbers via `snprintf("%.17g")`, tables via dkjson (already vendored), nil treated as zero-byte output. Variable-size outputs route through the large-value heap.
- Error handling: every `lua_pcall` checked; nonzero return prints `[lua spec] file:fn — error` and returns 1, which the dispatch layer turns into an abort (per 303).
- Makefile target for `spec.so`, links LuaJIT
- 10-step implementation sequence ending in smoke tests against existing maps

Open questions noted: nil semantics, multiple-return-value handling, table-to-JSON via dkjson vs inline serializer.

## Assistant

**Answers:**

**(1) Data boxes — what's their content?** Literal values, typed inline in the editor and stored verbatim in the box's `value` field of its JSON. At load time the loader allocates a slot for the data box and fills it with the bytes; consumers read it like any other slot. The editor offers a text input for inline typing and (eventually) a "load from file" button — but that button just reads the file and pastes the bytes into `value`, so what's in the map is always literal. No file I/O at runtime, no parsing inside the data box. JSON content goes in as a JSON string and is parsed downstream by a call box using `libs/json.lua`. Database queries, runtime file reads, anything dynamic — those are call boxes. Keeps `data` honest as a noun.

**(2) Fan-out and fan-in:** Both are already supported by the slot model.

- **Fan-out** (one output → many inputs): multiple wires reading from the same output slot. The slot's refcount equals the number of wires. Each downstream consumer `peek`s the slot (non-destructive read) and then `unref`s. The slot is freed when the last consumer unrefs. All consumers see the same single value.
- **Fan-in** (many outputs → one input): the destination input is a queued slot (multi-cell ring buffer, issue 213). Each producer task pushes its output value into the queue when it completes; the consumer pops in arrival order. Iterators are the typical fan-in consumer — they process one value per invocation across the queue.

**(3) Pool runner's main loop:** Mostly idle. After setup (load graph → start pool → init specs → submit entry boxes), the main thread blocks on a condvar tied to the active-task counter (issue 301). All actual work — box invocations, deferred allocator coalescing (issue 302), comparator/iterator routing — happens inside the worker threads as pool tasks. The deferred cleanup we discussed for 302 is submitted as just another task; a worker runs it, the counter increments and decrements like any task. When the counter hits zero, the main thread wakes and finalizes (writes `last-run.json`, destroys the pool, frees the graph). There is no separate "admin thread"; the runner is task-driven. Things you could add to the task stream over time: stats collection, periodic compaction, file-watch handlers — but for now, just box dispatch and allocator cleanup.

## Assistant

307 written.

Highlights:

- **Compile callback**: `fork` + `execlp("cc", ..., "-shared -fPIC -O2 -Wall")`. mtime-skip if the `.so` is newer than the source. `cc` (system default) — Linux gets gcc, macOS gets clang, no SoraMech-specific compiler dependency.
- **Per-worker handle**: a small dlopen-cache struct (`so_handles[]`, `fn_ptrs[]`). Same `.so` loaded once per worker; libc refcounts so the code segment is shared.
- **Box file convention**: fixed C signature via the `SM_BOX_ARGS` macro from a shipped `soramech-c.h`. Users write `int classify(SM_BOX_ARGS) { ... }` and call typed helpers like `sm_in_str(0)` / `sm_out_str("...")`. No libffi, no per-box codegen — just a header. Keeps the surface looking like ordinary C while the underlying ABI is uniform across boxes.
- **Input helpers**: `sm_in_str`, `sm_in_int`, `sm_in_double`, `sm_in_bool`, `sm_in_bytes` — parse the byte buffers into native C types. JSON inputs are not helper-supported (no native C JSON type); user includes their own JSON lib.
- **Output helpers**: `sm_out_str`, `sm_out_int`, `sm_out_double`, `sm_out_bool`, `sm_out_bytes`. Buffer overflow aborts (303 hard-crash).
- **Variable-size outputs**: `sm_out_alloc(n)` + `sm_out_handle(ptr, n)` via the large-value heap (302). Same shape as Lua's variable-size path; the helper hides it.
- **Thread safety**: same `.so` is shared across worker threads. Static variables and globals are racy. Documented as a user concern; `dlmopen` namespaces noted as a future option if races actually surface.
- **Build**: `langs/c/spec.c` links `-ldl`, nothing else. The user's C box files are compiled by the `compile` callback at map load, not by this Makefile.
- **Errors**: dlopen / dlsym / compile failures all print context to stderr and abort. No retry.

Open questions: per-box `cflags`, libffi-style typed signatures, dlmopen for static isolation — all deferred.

Implementation sequence is 11 steps from "stub spec.so" through full helper coverage and the `driver-test` map.

## Assistant

Let me address each comment, then update 307.

**Answers:**

**(1) cflags persistence** (line 77): The `cflags` field would live in the box JSON, just like `lang` / `ref` / `fn` / `inputs`. The editor saves whatever the user puts in the box's data, including this field — it doesn't overwrite it on save. Editor reads → user edits → editor writes back, with all fields preserved. The compile step reads the field at compile time.

**(2) JSON inputs vs the loader's JSON parser** (line 137): Two different JSON parsers, two different jobs.
- The graph loader (issue 305) uses a JSON parser to read the **map's structure** — the box files, data files, connection lists. That parser is internal to the runner; user code never sees it.
- A C box that wants to **parse a JSON string flowing through a slot** needs its own parsing step. We can wire that up: ship a `sm_in_json(i)` helper that parses the bytes into a `cJSON *` (using the same vendored cJSON the loader uses). The user navigates the resulting tree in their function. Updating the C spec to add this.

**(3) Users shouldn't have to edit box functions / accept any library function** (line 159): Significant change to the design. Updating to a wrapper-generation model: the user declares the function's signature in box JSON, the compile step generates a thin C wrapper that marshals SoraMech's bytes-in/bytes-out into the function's natural C arguments. The user can drop in any function — their own, or one from zlib / OpenCV / wherever — by declaring its signature, headers, and libraries to link. No `SM_BOX_ARGS` macro for the user. They write or reference natural C.

**(4) `static` and races** (line 194): `static` in C does not mean immutable — that is `const`. `static` at file scope means "this symbol has internal linkage" (only visible within the file); the variable itself is still mutable. `static` inside a function means "this variable persists across calls" (function-local static), still mutable. So `static int counter = 0;` in a C box is mutable shared state across worker threads, and races. Clarifying in the issue.

**(5) What `dlopen` / `dlsym` do** (line 216): `dlopen("path/to/file.so", flags)` loads a shared library at runtime — the OS loader maps the library's code into memory and returns a handle. `dlsym(handle, "name")` looks up a symbol (function or variable) in that loaded library by name and returns a pointer to it. Together: load a `.so` file, find a function in it by name, get a pointer you can call directly. Same shape as Windows' `LoadLibrary` + `GetProcAddress`. Plugin systems use this. Adding a brief explainer.

**(6) cflags field — where edited?** (line 242): An additional field in the box inspector. When a C-language box is selected, the inspector shows a "Compile flags" text input alongside `lang` / `ref` / `fn`. Phase 2 inspector work — flagging it for issue 222 / inspector enhancement.

**(7) libffi** (line 248): A library that lets you call a C function whose signature you don't know at compile time. You describe the signature at runtime ("two int args, returns int"), give it the argument values, and libffi handles the calling-convention details (which register goes where, stack alignment, etc.). Used by Python's `ctypes`, Ruby's `FFI`, etc. Skipping it in favor of compile-time wrapper generation: same effect, no runtime dependency.

**(8) dlmopen** (line 253): A Linux variant of `dlopen` that loads a library into an isolated "namespace" — each namespace gets its own copy of the library's symbols (including statics). Two threads calling functions in two different namespaces wouldn't share static-variable state. Less portable than `dlopen` (Linux-only). Mentioned only as a future fallback if static-variable races become a real problem.

## Assistant

**307 corrections applied**:
- Compile callback signature extended to take `box_t *` (small extension to 303). Wrapper generation lives in the C spec's compile.
- Box file convention rewritten: users do NOT use `SM_BOX_ARGS`. They write natural-signature C functions, or reference library functions with `headers` / `link_libs` declarations and no user `.c` file. The compile step generates a thin wrapper named `sm_box_entry` that marshals SoraMech's bytes to the function's typed args.
- Input marshalling table updated: `string` / `int` / `long` / `double` / `bool` / `bytes` / `json` (the `json` case now uses cJSON and the user gets a `cJSON *`).
- Output encoding updated: the wrapper reads the user's return value and serializes per the declared `returns` type. `bytes` returns get out-pointer pass-through for library functions like `compress`. `void` returns produce zero-byte output.
- Variable-size outputs handled at compile time based on `output_capacity = 0` — the wrapper picks the large-value heap path when the box declares variable-size.
- Thread-safety section now correctly explains `static` (internal linkage, still mutable) vs `const` (immutable). `dlmopen` explained as the fallback escape hatch.
- New "What dlopen and dlsym do" subsection in Build with plain-language explanation.
- Open questions resolved: `cflags` field added, libffi noted as the runtime alternative we're skipping in favor of compile-time wrappers, dlmopen kept as a deferred fallback.

**308 written — Bash language spec**:
- The "Option 3" socket-server model — proves the spec interface accommodates languages that can't be embedded.
- One persistent Bash subprocess per worker thread, addressed by `/tmp/soramech-<run-id>-bash-<N>.sock`. Subprocess stays alive for the whole run.
- Wire protocol: 4-byte big-endian length prefix + JSON body. Same format both directions. Op-code is `invoke` or `shutdown`.
- `bash-server.sh` is small: read framed request → `source` file (cached after first time, since Bash keeps function definitions across calls in the same shell) → call function → capture stdout → write framed response.
- Inputs are positional args (`$1`, `$2`, …). Output is the function's stdout. No type marshalling — Bash has one type, string.
- Idle cost ~3–5 MB per subprocess; 16 workers ≈ 50–80 MB total.
- Variable-size outputs handled at the spec.c side, not in Bash — large response bytes get copied into the large-value heap; the subprocess just sends the bytes.
- Error handling: any failure (subprocess dies, nonzero exit, bad protocol) crashes the program per 303.
- Open questions: `socat` vs bundled framing helper for the Bash side (Bash has no native length-prefix socket I/O); per-worker stderr capture without races.

Three plausible candidates, and I'd lean against priority queue first.

Phase 3 changes the shape of the problem — multiple boxes invoking concurrently across worker threads, iterators producing many invocations, possibly a single box running thousands of times. The naive "append a line per invocation" gets messy when threads are racing. We need a deliberate design: what gets logged, where it's buffered, how it's flushed, what the editor can render. - **Priority queue / task scheduling** — what SoraMech does with the 3d-rts pool's priority knob. There's a real question (should iterator-queue draining outrank new entry submission? should allocator cleanup run only when idle?) but it might be premature to design before we have a workload that hurts under uniform priority. - **Compile orchestration** — when does compile run, how does the graph walk drive each spec's `compile` callback. Honest answer: thin. 222 says when (button click), 307 says how (per spec). Probably doesn't justify its own issue.
- **Phase 3 integration test plan** — how we prove phase 3 works against phase 2. Test maps, parity, the phase demo. Could be its own issue but feels like a phase wrap-up doc rather than an architectural piece.

## Assistant

**On the sleep question:** A sleep inside a box function ties up one worker thread for the full duration of the sleep. The thread is sitting inside `lua_pcall` (Lua), or in a `dlsym`'d function (C), or in `read()` waiting on the Bash subprocess that's running `sleep`. It's suspended, doing nothing.

Concretely:
- 16-worker pool, 1 box sleeping 10s → 15 workers keep running. Fine.
- 16-worker pool, 16 boxes all sleeping → all workers parked, nothing else runs until a sleep finishes.
- The pool's CPU budget is also the budget for "blocking activities." Heavy sleep usage means the user bumps `SORAMECH_WORKERS`.

The cooperative alternative — without a scheduler — is graph-level: the user splits "kick off the thing" and "check if the thing is done" into two boxes wired through an iterator with a queued input, polling at the graph level. Each individual box returns quickly; the iteration handles the wait. Pattern, not infrastructure.

This is just documented behavior. Goes in `docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md` (already mentions blocking semantics) — not its own issue.

**Issue count from your decisions:**

Three confirmed:
- **309** — Build system & Makefile orchestration
- **310** — Priority queue (built per spec, every task priority 1, max priority 1; no-op behaviorally, but the mechanism is wired in for later)
- **311** — Phase 3 integration tests

Possible fourth, if you want it as its own issue rather than folded into 311 or 309:
- **312** — Run output / `last-run.json` format and writer (decoupled from editor; the viewer utility you mentioned would be a separate phase 4-or-later thing). The questions are real — what gets logged, how do concurrent worker threads write without races, what's the file format for LLM/CLI consumption — but they're not huge. Could fit inside 311's test plan since tests need to read the output.

So: **3 minimum, 4 if you want run output as its own design issue.** Your call.

All four artifacts done.

**309 — Build system & Makefile orchestration**:
- Top-level Makefile orchestrating the runner build and per-spec builds via `$(MAKE) -C langs/<name>/`
- Directory layout: `src/` for runner sources (split into pool-runner / slot-store / graph-loader / dispatch / spec-registry), `libs/task-pool/` and `libs/json/` vendored, `langs/<name>/` per spec, `build/` for intermediates
- Three build modes: release (default), `DEBUG=1`, `STRICT=1`
- Vendoring policy: copied source, no submodules, with `VENDOR.md` per directory
- Spec discovery at runtime: runner reads `/proc/self/exe`, finds `langs/` next to itself; `SORAMECH_LANGS_DIR` overrides
- macOS `.dylib`/`_NSGetExecutablePath` flagged in open questions

**310 — Priority queue: wired in, no-op behaviorally**:
- Every `pool_spawn` passes `SM_PRIORITY_DEFAULT = 1`; `SM_MAX_PRIORITY = 1`
- Reasoning: future hooks become config changes, not refactors
- Speculative future priority schema noted (cleanup at 0, urgent at 3, etc.) but explicitly out of scope
- Implementation is essentially "define two constants, use them everywhere"

**311 — Integration tests & run output (combined)**:
- **Part A** — Run output: switched format to **JSON Lines** (`last-run.jsonl`), one event per line. Append-only, thread-safe with a single dedicated writer thread consuming an MPSC ring buffer. Decoupled from editor (LLM, viewer utility, test harness consumers). Default verbosity logs task lifecycle (submit/start/end); `SORAMECH_LOG_VALUES` and `SORAMECH_LOG_SLOTS` opt-in to inputs/outputs and allocator events.
- **Part B** — Tests: 11 test maps covering hello / data-only / fan-out / fan-in / comparator / iterator-100 / multilang / large-output, plus three `*-fail` tests for cycle detection / missing input / box error. Test driver normalizes timestamps, durations, worker indices, slot/task IDs out and diffs against expected JSONL.
- Phase 3 demo map under `issues/completed/demos/phase-3/` with multiple entry boxes, all three languages wired together, iterator + comparator + large-output box.

**docs/004 update**: Added a "Blocking semantics for long-running operations" subsection explaining that `sleep` and blocking I/O occupy a worker thread for the duration; the cooperative graph-level pattern (split kick-off + check-done, wire through an iterator); pool size as the budget for blocking activities; explicit "no scheduler" stance.

That's all of phase 3 specified: 301–311.
