## Assistant

Here's a paragraph each on the editor UX issues, plus a note on 212 which looks like it's already done.

**202 — Fit-to-view on map load.** When a map opens, the camera stays at origin/zoom 1. Boxes saved at distant coordinates land off-screen and the user has no idea where to look. Fix: after `load_map()`, compute the bounding box of every box's UI position, center the camera on it, and pick a zoom that fits everything with ~60px margin (clamped to 0.25–1.5). Expose `fit_to_view()` as a toolbar button so the user can re-center any time. Touches `005-app.js`, `001-canvas.js`, `002-boxes.js`. Small, self-contained, mostly math. A good first one.

**203 — Map picker panel.** First-load currently shows a browser `prompt()` for the server URL and map name. Switching maps wipes localStorage and re-prompts. Replace with a real sidebar panel: a server URL text input, a list of available maps fetched from `GET /maps`, click to connect. The "switch map" button reopens the panel without wiping the server URL. Touches `005-app.js`, `003-api.js`, `index.html`. Medium; involves new sidebar state.

**204 — Visible interaction hints.** New users see a blank canvas and two toolbar buttons; every interaction (double-click to create, drag port to wire, space-drag to pan, scroll to zoom) is undiscoverable. Render a centered hint overlay on the canvas while the box list is empty (drawn in canvas-2D, not DOM, so it doesn't fight the camera transform). Update the bottom-left status bar to reflect interaction state ("drawing wire — release on an input port", "selected: <box-id>", etc.). No persistence — the hint just disappears once a box exists. Touches `005-app.js`, `001-canvas.js`. Small.

**206 — Entry box designation.** `meta.json` already has an `entry_box_id` field, but the editor doesn't display it or let you change it. You have to edit `meta.json` directly. Fix: render a "▶" badge in the entry box's header on the canvas; add a "set as entry" button at the top of the inspector that PUTs `meta.json` and redraws. Single entry box at a time. Touches `005-app.js`, `002-boxes.js`, `004-inspector.js`, `003-api.js`. Small.

**208 — Port literal values (and remove `outputs`).** Two things at once. First, every input port currently requires a wired connection — there's no way to supply a constant. Add an optional `value` string field per input port, editable in the inspector; if no wire is connected, the value is used. Wires win when both are present. Second, **input port names become read-only** (they're derived from the function signature via the file browser, issue 207); only the value is editable. Third, **`box.outputs` is removed** — every call box has one output wire (issue 218 already settled this; the inspector still shows an outputs section that needs to go). The output side becomes the comparator toggle from issue 210. Touches the schema, the executor, the inspector. Larger; it's adjacent to the graph-model corrections, so doing it first sets up 210 and 213 nicely.

**212 — Editor interaction modes.** *Heads up: the issue file says* `Status: complete` *internally, but it's still in `issues/`, not `issues/completed/`. The work is described as done — `+ box` and `✕ wire` toolbar tools, double-click creation removed, box deletion strips references first to avoid the server's 409. If the implementation is in `005-app.js` and `index.html` already, this just needs to be moved to `completed/` and the progress file fixed. Worth verifying against the actual code before picking it up.*

**215 — View source from the inspector.** When a box misbehaves, you have to find the `ref` path and open it in a separate editor. Add a "view source" button next to the `ref` field; clicking replaces the inspector content with a read-only `<pre>` showing the file contents, with a "← back" button to return. Also add the same option to the canvas right-click context menu when a box is selected. No editing — read-only only. Touches `004-inspector.js`, `005-app.js`, `003-api.js`, the index.html CSS. Small to medium.

**222 — Compile button placeholder.** A "Compile" button in the editor toolbar that does nothing yet. The point is to establish the UI layout before the phase 3 compile pipeline (issue 219) lands. Click handler logs "compile clicked" and that's it — but the button is in the right spot for when the backend wires up. Touches `index.html`, `005-app.js`. Tiny. Probably worth doing alongside whatever other toolbar work happens first (202's fit-to-view button, for instance).

**My pick:** start with **202 (fit-to-view)** as a warm-up — small, self-contained, gives you immediate visible feedback. Then **204 (interaction hints)** while you're already in the canvas/render-loop code. Then either **206 (entry box badge)** or **215 (view source)**, both small and focused on the inspector. Save **208** for last in this batch — it crosses into the schema and executor, and it sets up the graph-model phase 2 work (210, 213, 217, 221) that comes after.

## Assistant

Done. The new implementation:

**What's the same:**
- Public API (`Canvas.cam`, `world_to_screen`, `screen_to_world`, `mark_dirty`, `start_frame`, `end_frame`, `mouse_pos`)
- Pan: middle-click drag, or space+left-click drag
- Zoom: scroll wheel (up = in, down = out)
- Hit-test consumers (`Canvas.cam.zoom` reads still work)

**What's different:**
- Internal state is `zoom` + `pan_x`/`pan_y` in screen pixels, not a world-space `cam.x`/`cam.y`. Drawing transform is `translate(pan); scale(zoom)`. This makes every operation pure-screen-pixel or pure-world arithmetic, with the conversion isolated in two functions.
- Pan uses an anchor-and-delta model — `mousedown` records the pan state, every `mousemove` computes from that anchor, so no error accumulates over a long drag.
- Zoom math is direct: after multiplying zoom, solve `pan_x = m.x - w.x * zoom` so the world point under the cursor stays put.
- `setTransform(1,0,0,1,0,0)` reset at frame start — guarantees no leftover matrix state from a previous frame can affect this one.
- All my debug instrumentation (the `defineProperty` getter/setter, the console logs) is gone.

Reload (hard reload to bust cache — Ctrl+Shift+R). Test:

1. Scroll up — does it zoom in (boxes get bigger)?
2. Scroll down — zoom out?
3. Middle-click drag — does it pan?
4. Space + left-click drag — does it pan?
5. Left-click drag in empty canvas — does nothing happen? (correct)
6. Left-click drag on a box — does the box move? (correct)

Tell me what works and what doesn't.

## Assistant

Different code paths, different blast radius.

**top-left "switch map"** — `onclick="localStorage.clear(); location.reload()"` — wipes ALL localStorage (server URL, current map, history, whatever else) and hard-reloads the page. That drops you to the first-load flow, which currently is `prompt()` for server URL and `prompt()` for map name (the bare browser dialogs). Nuclear option.

**top-right map name click** — calls `show_map_picker()`, an in-app sidebar panel that lets you pick from a list of maps without wiping anything else. Server URL and history stay intact.

So the toolbar one exists as the "escape hatch when stuck" — if the in-app picker breaks, you can always click the toolbar one and start fresh. But day-to-day, clicking the map name on the right is the cleaner path.

This is also exactly the territory **issue 203 (map picker panel)** is supposed to clean up. Right now: first-time load is still `prompt()`, switch-map (top-left) is `prompt()` again. 203's intent is to make even the first-time load show the same in-app panel that map-name-click shows, and at that point the top-left "switch map" button becomes redundant and can probably go.

Want me to do 203 next? It's a natural follow-on — it kills the `prompt()` calls entirely and removes the redundant toolbar button. After that, only **204 (interaction hints)**, **208 (port literals)**, **215 (source viewer window)**, and **222 (compile button placeholder)** remain in the UX batch.

It works, but I'm realizing the average width function is logically flawed. There are likely to be many lines that are very short, and few that are very long. Do you have a better idea or should we just initialize to 100 character width, just to be compatible with the most systems?

## Assistant

You're right — average gets pulled down by all the blank lines, closing braces, and single-keyword lines, so the window ends up too narrow for the lines you actually need to read.

Three reasonable options:

1. **Max line length, clamped to [80, 120]**: fits everything in the file, but never absurdly wide and never absurdly narrow. One line of code, adaptive — a tiny helper file gets a smaller window, a long file caps at a sane upper bound. My favorite.

2. **Hardcoded 100 chars**: predictable, simple, matches common code-style limits. Doesn't adapt at all, which is fine — the user can resize.

3. **Some high percentile (e.g. 90th)**: fits 90% of lines, ignores extreme outliers. More clever but more code, and overkill for this.

I'd go with **(1)** — same code complexity as a hardcoded number, but adapts when the file is short. Something like:

```js
const max_len  = lines.reduce((m, l) => Math.max(m, l.length), 0);
const w_chars  = Math.max(80, Math.min(120, max_len));
const w        = w_chars * CHAR_W + 20;  // +20 for padding
```

If you'd rather just go with hardcoded 100, that's fine too — it's a reasonable place to land and we can revisit if it ever feels wrong. Which way?

## User

It looks great! Now, I'm thinking about syntax highlighting. It'd be nice to have. How do text editors and IDEs usually implement that sort of thing? Should we have like, syntax templates or something, that users can provide for their custom languages?

## Assistant

Good question — syntax highlighting is a real feature. Here's the landscape, tersely:

**The four common approaches**, simplest to most accurate:

1. **Regex-pattern lists** — TextMate / VS Code / Sublime grammar files. Per-language file is a list of `{ pattern, className }` rules. Tokenize line-by-line, wrap matches in spans, style spans with CSS. Loose accuracy (regex can't truly parse code), but cheap, fast, and very easy to write or modify. The de-facto standard for editor-class highlighting.
2. **Hand-rolled lexers** — Write a small tokenizer per language. Better accuracy than regex, but you write one per language and they grow.
3. **Tree-sitter** — Real incremental parser per language compiled from a grammar. Best accuracy, used by GitHub / Neovim / Atom. Heavy: each grammar is a `.wasm` blob, tens of KB to MB.
4. **Library** — Prism.js or highlight.js, ~50 KB gzipped, covers ~200 languages out of the box. Drop in, done. No per-language work for anything common.

**For SoraMech specifically**, the natural fit is a hybrid that mirrors the language-spec architecture:

- **Default**: highlight.js for instant coverage of Lua / C / Bash / Python / Rust / anything common. Pick the language by file extension, which we already have on `lang_spec_t.file_ext` (issue 303).
- **Per-language override**: each `langs/<name>/` directory can optionally include a `syntax.json` that the viewer prefers over highlight.js when present. Format is simple — just a list of regex rules with CSS class names. Lets users style their custom-language boxes the way they want without depending on highlight.js shipping a definition.
- **Fallback**: plain text if neither a syntax file nor a highlight.js definition matches.

That's three layers, each one optional. Most users get good highlighting from layer 1 with zero work; advanced users override per language; edge cases degrade gracefully.

A `syntax.json` for, say, Lua would look like:

```json
[
  { "pattern": "--\\[\\[[\\s\\S]*?\\]\\]", "class": "syntax-comment" },
  { "pattern": "--.*",                       "class": "syntax-comment" },
  { "pattern": "\"(?:\\\\.|[^\"])*\"",       "class": "syntax-string" },
  { "pattern": "\\b(local|function|end|if|then|else|elseif|return|do|while|for|in|nil|true|false)\\b",
    "class": "syntax-keyword" },
  { "pattern": "\\b\\d+(?:\\.\\d+)?\\b",     "class": "syntax-number" }
]
```

CSS:
```css
.syntax-comment { color: #6c72a0; }
.syntax-keyword { color: #4a9eff; }
.syntax-string  { color: #4caf7d; }
.syntax-number  { color: #ff8c42; }
```

**My take**: this is worth doing, but it's its own issue, not a 215 add-on. Want me to:

1. Close 215 as-is (no highlighting yet — viewer is functional plain-text)
2. Open a new issue (216-something — actually 216 is taken; next free is 223 or so) speccing out the highlighting design above
3. Move on to 208 (the last UX issue in the batch)
4. Then come back to highlighting whenever

Or do you want to go after highlighting now while this code is fresh?

Found it. In `unmake_variadic`, I capture the slot-0 name AFTER renaming the port, which means the connection-update target is the new name (`text`), not the old name (`text_0`). The rename does nothing because no connection has `to_input === "text"`. So the `text_0`-targeting connection record stays in the box, and when the user re-toggles variadic on, it finds the matching slot name and the wire reappears.

## Assistant

Several real issues here. Let me work through them:

**213 deferral**: yes, defer it. Phase 3 (issue 302) implements the queue-per-port model directly in C; the phase 2 Lua executor's queue work is throwaway. I'll mark 213 as won't-implement.

**Variadic toggle bug** — root cause: when a wire is created, `create_connection` does `API.get_box` (fresh fetch) → mutate → `Boxes.boxes[id] = freshly_fetched`. This **replaces the reference**. The inspector's `current_box` was set when the user clicked the box and now points at the stale pre-fetch object. So `make_variadic` walks `current_box.connections`, finds nothing (no wire there), and the rename does nothing. Then `save()` overwrites the server with stale data, dropping the wire silently. The rename "works" only after a second wire-create because by then the inspector has been re-shown via `auto_grow_after_set`'s `show(box)` call.

Fix: never replace cache references. Mutate `Boxes.boxes[id]` in place. `current_box === Boxes.boxes[id]` stays true.

**Variadic toggle should snip all wires** (per your spec): both directions clear wires. Cleaner, less corner-cases.

**Other**: I'll write up the wire-erase pointer-skip bug and the "view" button condition as new issues.

Let me explain the bug, then write up the self-loop issue.

**The bug:** It was a classic JavaScript-reference-vs-mutation issue. Two pieces of code were holding references to "the same box" but those references could silently get out of sync.

When you click a box, the inspector grabs the box object as `current_box`. The canvas-side cache, `Boxes.boxes[id]`, is the same object — same reference, both names point to the same JS object in memory.

When you then connect a wire, `create_connection` did this:
```js
const dst_box = await API.get_box(to_box_id);   // fetch a *fresh* object from server
dst_box.connections.push(conn);                 // mutate the fresh one
await API.put_box(to_box_id, dst_box);          // PUT
Boxes.boxes[to_box_id] = dst_box;               // replace cache
```

That last line was the culprit. `Boxes.boxes[id]` now points at a **different object** than `current_box` does. The canvas reads `Boxes.boxes[id]` and sees the new wire. The inspector reads `current_box` and doesn't.

So when you clicked the variadic toggle, `make_variadic` walked `current_box.connections` looking for a wire to rename — found nothing (the wire is on the *new* `dst_box` object, not on `current_box`). The rename did nothing. Then `save()` PUT the stale `current_box` to the server, which silently dropped the wire because the stale object had no record of it.

The reason connecting a *second* wire seemed to "fix" things: `auto_grow_after_set` had a fallback line — `if (current_box.id === box.id) show(box, ...)` — that adopted the new `box` as the new `current_box`. After the second wire, the inspector was finally pointing at the same object as the cache, and subsequent operations worked.

**The fix:** mutate the cached objects in place instead of replacing the reference. Every place that used to do `Boxes.boxes[id] = freshly_fetched` now does `cached.connections = ...` directly on the existing object. Both `current_box` and `Boxes.boxes[id]` keep pointing at the same object, and there's no way for them to drift. Plus, on PUT failure, we roll back the in-memory mutation so the state stays consistent with the server.

The variadic-toggle "snip all wires" change was a separate decision — it makes the toggle behave as "shape change clears wires" so the rename + auto-grow corner cases stop existing in the first place. Easier to reason about than the previous "rename slot 0's wire, drop higher slots' wires" asymmetry.

Now writing the self-loop issue:

## Assistant

228 is open. Let me also clarify the bug-explanation point you asked about — three takeaways worth keeping in mind:

**1. JavaScript reference identity matters when state is shared.** Two modules holding "the same box" need to literally hold the same object. The moment one of them does `Boxes.boxes[id] = newObject`, anyone else holding the old reference is stuck with stale data. That's true for any mutable shared state in JS.

**2. The "fetch fresh, mutate, replace cache" pattern looks safe but isn't.** It's a reasonable pattern when nobody else holds references to the cached object. The instant a second module retains a reference (the inspector's `current_box`), the pattern silently breaks consistency. The fix — mutate in place — keeps every reference pointing at the same object, but it gives up concurrency safety (other tabs can't have edited the box). For this project that trade is fine.

**3. The cascade was self-reinforcing.** The auto-grow path's "if current_box.id matches, show(box)" line was acting as a delayed reconciliation — it eventually fixed `current_box` but only on the next wire-create. So the bug appeared "intermittent": first wire-create after a fresh box looked broken, the second one quietly fixed everything. That kind of "second action fixes the first action's leftovers" pattern is a strong signal of stale-reference bugs.

Want to keep going? Remaining open: 216 (read-file box, small lib like `text.lua`), 219 (map compiler — phase 3 work), 221 (iterator box), and the new follow-ups (223–228). 221 is the next one I'd expect from your earlier sequence (210 → 217 → 213 → 221, and 213 is closed-as-superseded).

     - Important because the original world-space camera math was causing zoom-direction confusion
     - Final pattern:
     ```js
     // sx = wx * zoom + pan_x
     function world_to_screen(wx, wy) {
       return { x: wx * zoom + pan_x, y: wy * zoom + pan_y };
     }
     function screen_to_world(sx, sy) {
       return { x: (sx - pan_x) / zoom, y: (sy - pan_y) / zoom };
     }
     // start_frame uses translate(pan_x, pan_y); scale(zoom, zoom)
     // Wheel zoom: pan_x = m.x - w.x * zoom (zoom about cursor)
     // Backward-compatible cam getter exposes x/y/zoom
     ```

Fix: mutate cached objects in place, never replace references. Applied to `create_connection`, `delete_connection`, `sever_output_wires`, `update_target_connections`, and the literal-value-severs-wires path.

## Test plan

### 227 — view button hides without a ref
1. Add a new box (right-click → add box here).
2. Open the inspector — **only** "browse" should appear next to the ref field; no "view" button.
3. Click "browse", pick a function. The "view" button now appears.
4. Click in the ref field, delete the text. The "view" button vanishes again.

### 226 — sinks shed their output port
1. Open `maps/hello`. Click the "Write Result" box.
2. Click "browse", re-pick the `write_result` function.
3. Verify on canvas: no output dot on the right edge of that box.
4. Verify in inspector: no "compare" button, no output section at all.
5. Try wire-dragging from where the dot used to be — nothing happens (no port to grab).

### 228 — self-loop wires
1. On any box, drag from its output dot back to one of its own input dots.
2. The wire should arc **around the bottom** of the box (not slice through the body).
3. Click on the visible part of the wire — it should select (status bar shows the connection).
4. Press Delete — the wire goes away.
5. With a comparator box (set a comparand), self-loop one branch (e.g. `lt` back into the same box's input). The wire should still route around and keep its branch color.

### 221 — iterator box (editor surface)
1. New box. In inspector, click "iterator" next to the kind dropdown.
2. The ref/fn/output controls disappear; an "outputs (round-robin)" section shows with one slot named `output_0` and a "+ slot" button.
3. Wire from `output_0` to another box's input. A new placeholder slot `output_1` should auto-appear.
4. Wire `output_1` to a third box. `output_2` appears.
5. Rename `output_0` to `left` in the inspector — verify the connected wire is preserved (the connection's branch label updates).
6. Click `×` on `output_2` — slot disappears, no leftover wires.
7. Click "iterator ×" to toggle off. All output wires get severed; ref/fn rows return.

### 216 — read-file library
1. In the file browser, add `libs/` as a library directory if it isn't already shown.
2. Open `files.lua`. You should see two functions: `read_text` and `read_data`.
3. Pick one to populate a box. It works as any other Lua box.
4. (Optional) Read `libs/files.lua.info.md` for the API.

### 225 — dir picker
1. In the file browser, click "+ library dir" to open the directory picker.
2. Verify each row is prefixed: `> name/` for directories (blue), `· name` for files (dim gray).
3. The parent `..` row is also a `>` dir row.
4. Navigate into a folder containing source files — those files appear listed (non-clickable) below the dirs.
5. An empty directory shows "(empty directory)".

### 224 — editable port names (canvas overlay)
1. Select a box with input ports.
2. Each input port has a small text field floating next to its dot on the canvas.
3. Click into one, type a new name, press Enter (or click away). The name updates everywhere.
4. If a wire was connected to that port, the wire stays connected after rename (check by clicking the wire and seeing the new `to_input` in the status bar).
5. Try a name with a space or starting with a digit — should be rejected with a status-bar error and revert.
6. Try renaming to a name that already exists on the same box — also rejected.
7. Pan and zoom the canvas — the overlay text follows the box, but text size stays legible (doesn't scale with zoom).
8. Press Escape while editing — the field reverts.

### 223 — syntax highlighting
1. Click "view" on a Lua box (e.g. write-result).
2. The viewer should now show colors: keywords blue, strings green, numbers orange, comments dim italic, identifiers white.
3. Open a `.sh` file (if any) — same shape, Bash keywords highlighted, `$var` / `${...}` colored as identifiers.
4. Open a `.c` file — same shape; `//` and `/* */` comments dim, `#include`/`#define` lines tagged.
5. Open a file with an unknown extension — it should still display as plain text (no error).

### Cross-feature regressions to watch
- **Variadic inputs** (the concat box): `var` toggle still works, slots auto-grow on connect, `×` removes slots. The new editable-name overlay shouldn't break the `text_0`, `text_1`, … pattern.
- **Comparator branches**: `compare` toggle still produces lt/eq/gt with the right colors. Toggling it severs old wires correctly.
- **File browser**: clicking a file in the main list (not the dir picker) still opens the function list; the `·` prefix is cosmetic and the click still works.

## Assistant

Done. Bugfixes shipped. Now your other questions:

**Font scaling on port labels** — fixed automatically by reverting to `ctx.fillText`; canvas-rendered text scales with the camera.

**Variadic slot removal renaming others** — fixed: no more compaction. Slots keep their existing indices when you remove a middle one. Auto-grow uses "highest existing index + 1," so gaps are harmless.

**Variadic toggle off not severing all wires** — fixed: now severs every incoming wire to the box (the previous code only matched wires whose port still fit `<base>_<N>`, so any slot you'd renamed escaped the sweep).

**Variadic auto-grow sometimes not firing** — possibly related to the rename issue: a variadic slot renamed off-pattern stops counting toward `last_variadic_index`, and auto-grow then fires on a different slot than the user expects. Now that variadic slot names are disabled in the inspector (the field grays out), the pattern stays intact and auto-grow should fire reliably. If you can repro after this commit, let me know the exact sequence.

**Syntax highlighting + external editor refresh** — `open_source_view` re-fetches every time you click "view," so closing and re-clicking already shows the freshest content. You're right that the editor never *runs* the source — that happens at compile / interpret time, not at view time. The view button is the one path that needs current bytes, and it already does. No change needed.

**Data boxes** — your read of them is more correct than the implementation. Right now the `data` kind is purely cosmetic: green badge, otherwise treated identically to `call` boxes by both schema and loader. There's no "read a file and output its contents" semantics — that's exactly what `libs/files.lua` (issue 216) now does, via a regular call box. So the `data` kind as it stands is a leftover category that doesn't earn its keep. Two reasonable next moves:

1. **Drop it.** A call box pointing at `files.read_text` covers the whole "load a file, emit a string" pattern, which is what you're describing. Aligned with no-globals — every file load is explicit and visible in the graph.
2. **Repurpose it** as a dedicated read-only file source (no fn, just a path; runtime emits file contents). Saves a couple of clicks but doesn't add capability `files.read_text` lacks.

I'd lean (1) — fewer concepts, and the existing data files used by `soramech-data` are fine as ordinary library calls. Want me to open an issue to remove the kind?

**Inspector layout** — done in this commit. Two-row port block: name + variadic toggle on top, full-width value below. Names show on the canvas (read-only there) and edit in the inspector. Box body stays click-through for drag.

## Assistant

Done. Now your variadic questions and confirmations:

**Variadic-input detection from source.** Currently it's the file browser's parser in `007-filebrowser.js`, plain JS — not a language spec.

- **Lua**: `parse_lua` matches `function M.name(p1, p2, ...)` and pulls each comma-separated param as one input port. The literal Lua variadic `...` shows up as a port literally named `...`; clicking "var" on it renames to `args_0` (the base becomes `args`).
- **Bash**: `parse_bash_inputs` scans for `local var="$N"` and bare `$N` references, takes the highest N as the arg count. `"$@"` / `$#` aren't detected — user marks the port variadic manually.
- **C**: no parser yet (lexer ships in 223 for highlighting only).

So variadic-ness is **a user tag**, not a parsed-from-source signal. The "var" button records the user's intent in `box.variadic_inputs`.

**Defining it in a language spec (phase 3, issue 303).** The natural extension is for each language spec to expose a `parse_signature(source) → { inputs, outputs, variadic_base? }` callback. The Lua spec would map `...` to `variadic_base = 'args'`; the Bash spec would map `"$@"` to `variadic_base = 'args'`; the C spec would map `va_list` / `...` similarly. The editor would auto-flip the variadic toggle when the parser reports a variadic tail. None of that exists yet — phase 2's parser is hand-rolled in JS, and the user's "var" toggle is the source of truth.

**Provider provides MORE values than expected.** Doesn't happen in the current model. Each wire carries one value; a variadic group is N wires into N distinct slots. There's no "spread" — a source box can't fan one value out into multiple variadic slots. If you want fan-in to a variadic group, you wire N sources to the N slots manually.

**Provider provides FEWER (some slots empty).** A slot with no wire and no literal value resolves to `nil` on the Lua side (or empty string on the Bash side). The receiving function gets the empty/nil arg. Whether that's a runtime error depends on the function — `text.concat("\n", a, nil, c)` works fine; a function that does `string.upper(b)` with `b=nil` errors.

**Strict-mode for missing args**: doesn't exist. Worth opening if it bites — the natural place is the dispatch layer (issue 304), which already validates input presence per the per-task slot store (issue 302).

---

**Confirmation on input port renames** — yes, gone. Names are read-only labels in the inspector now (just committed). The `rename_port` helper is removed, the variadic-disabled gymnastics gone too. The "var" toggle still owns slot naming; user-driven freeform rename is no longer a concept.

**229 opened** — data-box-as-language-agnostic-file-source, with a paired write sink. Here's the actual lay of the land.

## Where the language-specific code lives

**Wrong place** (in the editor, JS, not in `langs/`):
- `assets/js/007-filebrowser.js::parse_lua` — extracts `function M.name(args)` and scans for `return` statements.
- `assets/js/007-filebrowser.js::parse_bash` — extracts function definitions and `$N` references.
- Driver scripts in `drivers/lua.sh` / `drivers/c.sh` / `drivers/bash.sh` — shell-out to launch each language. These move out at phase 3 (specs replace drivers).

**Right place** (`langs/<name>/`):
- `langs/lua/lexer.js`, `langs/bash/lexer.js`, `langs/c/lexer.js` — just shipped (issue 223).
- Phase 3's `langs/<name>/spec.so` — runtime invocation contract.

The file-browser parsers are the violation. They should move to `langs/<name>/parser.js`, mirroring the lexer pattern. Worth opening an issue.

## Var toggle as user truth, language spec as interpreter

Right — leave the toggle as the user's intent. The translation from "this group is variadic, here are slots `args_0, args_1, args_2`" to a language-native variadic call lives in the **dispatch layer** (issue 304), not the spec. Dispatch:

1. Reads variadic_inputs from the box JSON.
2. Walks `box.inputs` collecting slot values in order: `["args_0_value", "args_1_value", "args_2_value"]`.
3. Hands the spec a flat array of values in declared-input order. The spec doesn't know "variadic" exists — it just calls the function with N positional args.

How each language receives N positional args is its own problem:
- **Lua**: function declared with `function f(a, b, ...)` — Lua's `...` collects them.
- **Bash**: positional args `$1 $2 $3 ...` — naturally variadic.
- **C**: needs either a generated wrapper (`compile` callback in issue 307) that takes a count + array, or `va_list`. The wrapper is per-box, generated at compile time.

So the spec interface stays simple: "here's a list of byte buffers, invoke." Variadic-ness is dispatch-layer accounting.

## Cross-language values and missing args

**Phase 2 wire format**: JSON strings on the command line. Structured values JSON-encoded; the driver decodes per its language. Output is one JSON value on stdout.

**Phase 3 wire format**: byte buffers via `invoke(input_data[], input_sizes[], n_inputs, out_buf, ...)` per `lang_spec_t`. The bytes are still naturally JSON — that's what's already in flight from phase 2 — but the spec API doesn't mandate it; it just says "byte buffers." The convention is JSON.

**Missing values**: a slot with no wire and no literal has no JSON to encode. Two reasonable answers:
1. **Don't fire the task** until all required inputs are present. This is what the per-task slot store (issue 302) is built for — the dispatch task waits for all wires to deliver.
2. **Fire with `null`** for missing slots, let the language map JSON null to whatever (Lua nil, Bash empty string, C `NULL`).

The right one depends on whether the missing-ness is intentional ("optional input, use default") or accidental ("forgot to wire"). The current phase 2 executor effectively does (2) — the box runs whether or not all slots are connected, missing slots become nil in Lua. Phase 3 should make this explicit per port: a port carries an `optional: true` flag; without it, missing wires stall the task.

**Cross-language translation**: each spec's `invoke` receives bytes the dispatch layer produced from upstream. Lua emits JSON of a number, Bash receives the same JSON string, parses it, treats it however Bash treats numbers (it doesn't — they're all strings, which is fine). The wire format (JSON) is the lingua franca; each language is responsible for converting JSON ↔ native at its boundary. **Null** specifically: JSON null on the wire, each language maps to its own bottom value.

The Bash subtle case: Bash has no concept of null — it has empty string and unset variable. Convention: JSON null → unset, JSON empty string → empty. The Bash spec (issue 308) needs to settle that.

## Suggested next issues

Three small ones, all phase 2:

1. **Move `parse_lua` and `parse_bash` from `007-filebrowser.js` into `langs/<name>/parser.js`**. Same lazy-import pattern as the lexers. The file browser asks the per-language parser for `{ inputs, outputs, variadic_base? }`. Auto-flips the var toggle when the parser reports a variadic tail.
2. **Optional-input flag on ports**. Schema accepts `port.optional: true`; dispatch layer (phase 3) treats unset optional as JSON null, unset non-optional as a stall.
3. **Document the wire format and cross-language null/missing handling** in `docs/004-ipc-and-threading.md` — currently the implicit convention isn't written down.

## Assistant

## Dispatch layer × threading

The pool (issue 301) holds N worker threads. Each worker has a small per-language state cache — one `lua_State`, one set of `dlopen`'d C symbol tables, one persistent Bash subprocess — initialized at pool startup so language launches don't repeat per call.

A **task** is one box invocation. Tasks live in the pool's queue. When a worker is free, it pulls the front task and runs the dispatch action against it.

The dispatch action is one C function, ~200 lines. Its phases:

1. **Check inputs ready.** Walk the input slots. If any slot has no value yet, the action returns `ACT_BLOCK` and the pool parks the task on that slot's wait list. The slot owns the wait list, not the wire — when an upstream task pushes a value into the slot, it walks the wait list and wakes everyone parked there.
2. **Read inputs into byte buffers.** The slot store hands back the bytes; the language spec doesn't see slot IDs.
3. **Branch on box mode.** Plain call → invoke the spec. Comparator → compare input to threshold, produce a branch tag, no spec call. Iterator → copy input straight to output, no spec call.
4. **Invoke** (plain call only). The spec turns bytes into native types, calls the user function, serializes back to bytes.
5. **Write output** to the box's output slot. Slot store walks the slot's wait list and wakes parked tasks.
6. **Unref consumed inputs.** Single-value slots are reference-counted per fan-out wire; queued slots persist (iterators re-pop across invocations).
7. **Route.** Plain call fires all outgoing connections; comparator fires only the matching branch; iterator fires the counter-th branch and respawns a successor task with `counter+1`.
8. **Return ACT_DONE.** Pool decrements the active-task count.

So the threading is: many tasks queued, M < N tasks running in parallel on the pool's workers, each task is one dispatch-action invocation. Blocking-on-input is cooperative — the action returns ACT_BLOCK and the worker is freed for other work; when the slot fills, the parked task is requeued. No thread sleeps on a slot, no spinlocks, no per-box thread.

Boxes don't map to threads. Workers are uniform; any worker can run any box.

---

## No null

Agreed — and the compile button is the natural enforcement point. Three rules:

1. Every input port must have a wire OR a literal value OR `optional: true` on the port.
2. `optional: true` ports without a wire and without a literal don't fire — the dispatch action treats them as "not present" and the receiving function never sees a null. Languages get a way to ask "is this arg present?" at the spec boundary; absent means absent.
3. Compile fails loudly when an input violates rule (1), pointing at the box and the port.

The Bash null-vs-empty quagmire goes away because absent never reaches Bash — it just doesn't get the variable set, and the spec sees zero positional args for that slot. Lua: missing `optional` arg is `nil` only because Lua's calling convention has no other choice; but that's a language-internal artifact, not a wire-protocol value. Nothing crosses the wire as null.

Worth opening as an issue — it has a real schema change (the `optional` flag), a compile-button hookup, and per-spec "absent argument" semantics. Want me to?

---

## Variadic, illustrated

Picture a box on the canvas as a small machine with input plugs on its left side. A normal port is a single plug — one wire goes in. The function on the other end of that plug has a parameter named, say, `text`, and the wire's value lands there.

A variadic port is **a strip of plugs that grows as you use it**. You start with one plug, just like a normal port. You wire something to it. The moment you make that connection, a fresh empty plug appears below, ready for the next wire. Wire that one too, and another empty plug appears. The box keeps the plugs you've wired and always has one empty one waiting at the bottom.

The function on the other end isn't seeing N separate parameters — it's seeing **one parameter that's a bundle of values**. In Lua, that's `function f(...)` and inside the function you've got a list. In Bash, that's `"$@"` — all the positional arguments, however many were passed. The variadic strip on the canvas is the visual representation of "stuff goes into this collection."

Why bother? Because the alternative — declaring up front "this function takes exactly 5 strings" — locks you into 5. A concat box should join however many strings you've got. A logging box should accept however many fields you want to log. A list-builder should take however many items you've got items. Variadic is the editor saying "I don't know how many you'll wire, and I won't make you guess."

The "var" toggle in the inspector is the user telling the editor "this port is the strip kind, not the single-plug kind." The slots `args_0`, `args_1`, `args_2` are how the box JSON tracks the individual plugs (each one a normal connection point with a unique name, so wires can find the right one). The dispatch layer reads the strip in order, hands the values to the function as a flat list, and the language's native variadic syntax does the rest.

When you remove a slot, the strip just gets a hole where that plug used to be — the others keep their names. Auto-grow uses "highest existing slot + 1," so holes are harmless. The toggle off collapses the strip back to one plug; the slots and any wires attached to them go away, because the shape changed and the wires no longer mean what they used to.

Three sources of truth, in order:
1. **The user**, via the var toggle. They mark a port as the strip kind.
2. **The dispatch layer**, which knows how to walk a strip and produce a flat ordered argument list.
3. **The language spec**, which knows how to push a flat ordered argument list into its native variadic call convention.

Each layer does one thing. None of them needs to know what the others know.

## Assistant

Lots to answer. Let me work through them:

## How users define language specs

A spec is a small C file that implements `lang_spec_t` (issue 303):

```c
typedef struct {
    const char *name;        // "lua"
    const char *file_ext;    // "lua"
    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);
    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
    int   (*compile)(...);   // optional — only languages that need it
    int   (*invoke)(void *handle, const char *file_path, const char *fn_name,
                    const void **input_data, const int *input_sizes, int n_inputs,
                    void *out_buf, int out_buf_capacity, int *out_size);
} lang_spec_t;
```

The user writes `langs/<name>/spec.c`, `langs/<name>/Makefile` (which builds to `spec.so`), and exports a global `soramech_lang_spec` of type `lang_spec_t`. The pool runner `dlopen`s every `langs/<dir>/spec.so` at startup and indexes by `name` and `file_ext`. Adding a language is: create the directory, write `spec.c`, write `Makefile`, drop in.

The init/teardown pair is where per-worker state lives — the `lua_State`, the `dlopen`'d C symbol table, the persistent Bash subprocess. Each worker thread calls `init` once at pool startup; the returned `handle` is stored in worker-thread-local state and passed back to `invoke` on every call.

## Workers pull, no orchestrator

Already the design — main thread loads the graph, creates the pool, submits the entry tasks, then sleeps on a condition variable until the active-task counter hits zero. After that point, **everything is worker-driven**: each worker pulls one task off the pool's queue, runs the dispatch action, and either:
- Submits successor tasks (regular completion).
- Parks the task on a slot's wait list (input not ready).
- Re-submits itself to advance the iterator counter.

Slot-fill from a completing task wakes parked tasks by re-adding them to the queue — done by the same worker that pushed the value, no orchestrator involved. The main thread only re-enters operation at quiescence (counter == 0) to tear down the pool.

## "Waking" = re-add to ready queue

Yes. Each parked task is just a `dispatch_task_t*` sitting on a slot's wait list. The slot-push code walks the list, takes each pointer off, and `pool_spawn`s it (or whatever the equivalent re-enqueue is in the 3d-rts pool API). The task lands at the back of the ready queue and gets picked up by whichever worker is free next.

## Comparator compares OUTPUT, not input — you're right

Issue 304's text is wrong here. The editor's actual model (and what makes sense): a comparator is a **modifier on a call box**, not a separate routing primitive. The function still runs. Its output is compared to the box's `comparand`. The matching `lt` / `eq` / `gt` branch fires.

So phase 4 (invoke) runs for comparator boxes too. Phase 5 writes the function's output to the slot. Phase 7 (routing) then compares the output to the threshold and fires the matching branch — and only that branch.

I'll update 304's text to match. The editor is the source of truth here; the issue file drifted.

## Slot-store walk happens on the producing thread

The thread that just completed phase 5 (write output). After `slot_push`, the same worker walks the wait list, removes the parked tasks, re-submits them. Then it falls through to phase 6 (unref) and 7 (routing), then returns ACT_DONE and pulls the next task from the queue. No handoff to a coordinator.

## Refcount explanation

A slot is a small struct with the value bytes and a refcount. The refcount represents **"how many consumers haven't read this yet"** — it's a liveness count, not a producer count.

- When a task is submitted with this slot as one of its inputs, the slot's refcount goes up by 1 (one wire's worth of "I'm going to need this").
- When that task reaches phase 6 (unref consumed inputs), it decrements the count by 1.
- When the count reaches 0, no consumer has any further interest, and the slot is freed.

Fan-out works naturally: if three tasks are wired to the same upstream slot, the refcount starts at 3 (one per wire). Each task reads the value and unrefs; when the third task unrefs, the slot is freed. The order doesn't matter.

Queued slots (iterator inputs) are different — they're ring buffers. Each `slot_pop` drains one cell, but the slot itself stays alive because the iterator hasn't ended yet. The refcount on a queued slot tracks **producer liveness**: each upstream wire holds 1 ref while its producing box hasn't ended. When all producers have ended AND the iterator's own ref drops, the queue is freed.

## Optional inputs and "NULL" as a literal

You're right about both halves. Two cases:

1. **Required input is missing** → compile-time rejection. The user has to wire it, give it a literal, or mark it optional. No runtime null.
2. **User explicitly wants the value `null`** (because some library demands it). Allowed — they type the literal string `null` (or whatever convention we settle on). That's a string on the wire, just like `42` or `hello`. The receiving language gets the string `null` and decides what to do with it.

Your switch-on-type idea is the right shape. The wire format is JSON, which has a type tag built in (`"foo"` is a string, `42` is a number, `null` is null). The spec's `invoke` decodes the JSON byte buffer and translates per language:

```
JSON value      Lua             Bash             C
-----------     -----           ----             ----
"foo"           "foo"           "foo"            "foo"
42              42              "42"             42
true            true            "true"           1
null            nil             ""               NULL
```

(Bash gets every value as a string regardless — that's how Bash works.)

So: the wire carries typed JSON, the spec translates to the language's natural form. We don't ban null from the wire — we just ban null from being **the result of a missing input**. If the user explicitly wires a literal `null`, that's a real value, fine. If the user forgot to wire, that's a compile error, not a silent null injection.

The "is this arg present?" API at the spec boundary becomes: it isn't. Optional + absent = the spec gets a shorter arg list, period. The function's parameter falls into its language's "this parameter wasn't passed" handling (Lua: nil; C: must be the trailing variadic; Bash: not set). That is a separate semantics from "the value passed was null."

So the rule:
- **Absent** = arg position not in the call.
- **null** = arg position is in the call, value is the language's null.

These are different at the language level, and the spec respects the difference.

## Assistant

Three good questions. Let me work through them.

## How the spec interprets bytes (and JSON)

The spec is **the language ↔ bytes boundary**. The dispatch layer hands `invoke` a list of `(byte_pointer, byte_size)` pairs and a buffer to write the output bytes into. What's IN those bytes is the spec's choice and the spec's convention.

**Convention is JSON** because it's interoperable: every shipped language has a JSON parser, and JSON's type tags (string/number/bool/null/object/array) survive the trip across language boundaries.

In practice, each spec implements `invoke` like:

```c
// Lua spec, sketch
int lua_invoke(handle, file_path, fn_name,
               input_data[], input_sizes[], n_inputs,
               out_buf, out_buf_capacity, out_size) {
    lua_State *L = handle;
    luaL_loadfile(L, file_path);
    lua_getfield(L, -1, fn_name);

    for (int i = 0; i < n_inputs; i++) {
        // each input is a JSON value as bytes; parse into a Lua value
        cjson_decode_to_lua(L, input_data[i], input_sizes[i]);
    }

    lua_pcall(L, n_inputs, 1, 0);

    // serialize return value back to JSON bytes
    *out_size = cjson_encode_from_lua(L, -1, out_buf, out_buf_capacity);
    return 0;
}
```

The Bash spec is simpler — it passes the bytes as positional args (`"$1"`, `"$2"`, …) and reads stdout as the return JSON. The C spec compiles a per-box wrapper at `compile` time that knows the function's declared types and emits the right marshalling code.

The dispatch layer never inspects the bytes. The slot store explicitly says "no type tags" — it stores opaque bytes only. The wire format is a **pact between specs**, not a runtime invariant. Two specs that want to skip JSON and pass native bytes can do so — it's just that defaulting to JSON means any pair of specs can talk.

## Fast producer, slow consumer — what happens to the values?

Nothing is lost. The buffering happens **in the task queue**, not in any one slot.

The model is "one slot per invocation, not one slot per wire." If your upstream fires 10 times, that's 10 dispatch tasks, each one allocating its own output slot, each one pushing its value, each one firing its outgoing connections. Firing a connection means **spawning a downstream task** with that specific upstream slot as input. So if upstream A produces 10 outputs, downstream B has 10 tasks queued — each one paired with one of A's slots as its input.

The pool's ready queue holds those 10 B-tasks. If B is 10× slower than A, all 10 will sit in the queue (or be in flight on different workers, if there's parallelism), and B's worker pulls them one at a time. Each B-task reads its own slot, runs, produces its own output slot. No values overwrite each other — each producer-consumer pair has its own pair of slots.

So no, you don't need a ring buffer for the plain 1-cell case. The task queue **is** the rate buffer. Values become tasks, tasks queue, consumers drain tasks at their pace.

The ring-buffer slots are reserved for **iterator inputs** specifically: many upstream producers fanning into one consumer, where the consumer reuses the same slot across its own re-spawns. That's a different topology — fan-in to a long-lived consumer — and it needs the queue to survive across the consumer's invocations.

If a non-iterator B has multiple upstream wires and one upstream is fast while another is slow, things get more interesting: B can't fire until both inputs are ready. So a B-task spawned for upstream A's invocation N has to wait for the slow upstream's matching invocation N. The pairing is by task, not by slot — the dispatch layer's connection-firing logic figures out which task gets which inputs. (This is where the design gets harder; for current scope it's "fast producer + slow consumer with a single wire" which works cleanly.)

## Wire format — JSON, allocation, pointers

The slot is just bytes. Per 302's slot layout, each cell is a `cell_capacity`-byte region preceded by a 4-byte filled-size. The producer writes raw bytes there. The consumer reads raw bytes from there. **No JSON parsing happens at the slot level** — that's all in the spec.

So in concrete terms, when an upstream Lua box outputs `42`:
1. Lua's `invoke` runs, computes the Lua number `42`.
2. The spec's serializer turns `42` into the JSON byte string `"42"` (4 bytes including quotes if it's a string, 2 bytes `42` if it's a number — depends on the spec's chosen encoding).
3. Those bytes go into `out_buf`.
4. The dispatch action calls `slot_push(slot, out_buf, *out_size)` — the slot store memcpys into the cell.
5. Downstream Lua box's `invoke` runs. The dispatch action `slot_peek`s into a stack buffer.
6. The spec's deserializer parses the bytes back into a Lua number.

So yes — **encode + decode per hop**. That's the cost of language-agnosticism.

**Re-creating the JSON each time** — that part's true and worth being clear-eyed about. The per-hop cost is "one serialize + one deserialize per consumer." For LLM-call boxes (millisecond-to-second range) it's lost in the noise. For tight numeric loops it would matter, but we don't have that use case.

**Memory:** each slot is its own allocation. The slot store has a size-class allocator — boxes that consistently produce 64-byte outputs all draw from the 64-byte free list, so allocation is a list pop, not a malloc. For variable-size payloads (LLM responses, dynamic arrays), the slot holds a small `{size, offset}` handle into a separate **large-value heap**, and the bytes live there. So small values are inline in the slot (no pointer chase); large values are pointer-chased once.

**No re-editing.** Each `slot_push` is a single memcpy into a fresh cell. No accumulation, no rewriting. The slot lives until its refcount drops to zero, then its memory goes back to the free list.

**Pointers vs values:** the **spec API** never sees slot IDs — it only sees `const void *input_data[i]` and `void *out_buf`. The dispatch layer translates between slot IDs (which are how wires reference the data) and byte pointers (which is what specs work with). So at the spec boundary it's always pointers + sizes. At the wire/graph level it's slot IDs. The slot store is the indirection layer.

**Bypass for hot paths (future):** if it ever matters, two same-language adjacent boxes can negotiate a non-JSON encoding via the spec — e.g., the Lua spec could detect "upstream is also Lua" and skip JSON in favor of `lua_dump`/`lua_load` for table values. That's an optimization, not part of the contract; the slot store and dispatch layer wouldn't change.

If high-throughput numeric pipelines become a real workload, the right move is probably:
- **Type-annotated wires** at compile time (the box graph already records port types).
- **Per-type wire encodings** the specs can dispatch on — primitive numbers go raw, strings as length-prefixed bytes, complex types as JSON.

Worth its own issue if it becomes a concern. For now, JSON-as-default is the simplest correct thing.

Three big questions. Let me dig in.

## Slot lifecycle and the late-firing producer

The model is **slots are pre-allocated at graph load time, not at runtime**. The pool runner walks the static graph at startup and:

1. For every non-iterator box, allocates **one** task struct AND its output slot. Both exist before any task runs.
2. The task's `input_slots[]` array points at the upstream producer boxes' pre-allocated slots — slot identity is fixed at compile time for the static portion of the graph.
3. Every task (entry-point or not) is **submitted** to the pool at startup. Tasks whose inputs aren't ready immediately ACT_BLOCK on whichever input slot isn't filled yet, and the pool parks them on that slot's wait list.

So in your A/B/C scenario with B firing 30 tasks later: at program start, the loader allocates A_prod's slot, B_prod's slot, and C_task's slot. C_task is submitted, runs, finds A's slot empty, blocks on A's wait list. A_prod runs, pushes to A's slot, walks A's wait list, wakes C_task. C_task re-runs from the top, finds A's slot ready now but B's empty, blocks on B's wait list. Tens of tasks of unrelated work happen. Eventually B_prod runs, pushes to B's slot, walks B's wait list, wakes C_task. C_task re-runs, all inputs ready, invokes the function, writes output, fires its connections. 

The key answer to your "where does the slot live before B has fired" question: **the slot exists from the moment the graph loads**. It's just empty (head == tail). The wait list on it is what holds C_task. B_prod's output_slot pointer is also fixed at load time — it's pointing at the same pre-allocated memory C_task is waiting on.

This works because the graph is static for non-iterator parts. The compile-time enumeration is what makes the size-class allocator efficient in 302 — every distinct slot size is known at startup, and slots get drawn from the right free list with no allocation-on-the-hot-path.

**Iterators are the dynamic part.** An iterator that runs 10 times produces 10 invocation-slots. Each iterator invocation spawns a fresh successor task and a fresh output slot. Downstream of an iterator, the per-invocation tasks are spawned dynamically as well, paired with their corresponding iterator invocation.

Your "what if A produces between 1 and 50 values" is the iterator case. Yes — N iterator invocations produce N slots, fire N branches, spawn N downstream tasks. If those downstream tasks have other inputs from a *different* iterator, the pairing problem is real and unresolved in the current design — it's the open question I waved at last time. Probably needs a "synchronized iterator pair" concept or per-invocation indices that the dispatch layer can match. Worth an issue when we hit it.

**Why iterators get queued ring buffers and non-iterators don't**: an iterator has one input slot that survives across all its invocations. Multiple upstream producers may push values into that one slot before the iterator gets around to processing them — that's the rate-buffer case where the queue helps. A non-iterator box has one task, one output slot, runs once, done — no queue needed because there's only one push per slot ever.

## Where the dispatch layer runs

The dispatch layer is **not a thread**. It's a single C function (`dispatch_action`) that **runs on whichever worker pulled the task**. Every task in the pool is "dispatch this box" — when a worker pulls a task off the queue, it calls `dispatch_action(task)` on its own thread, runs through the phases (read inputs, invoke spec, write output, fire connections), and returns. The action lives entirely on the worker's stack.

So:
- The pool has N worker threads, each running its own loop: pull task, run dispatch_action, repeat.
- The dispatch action runs N-way in parallel across workers. Two boxes can be invoked simultaneously on different workers; they only contend on shared slot memory (per-slot lock).
- The "main" thread is asleep on a condition variable waiting for active task count to reach zero. It does no orchestration.
- Connection-firing (spawning a downstream task) happens **inside the dispatch action** on the producer's worker. The worker calls `pool_spawn(downstream_task)` — that just enqueues the task onto the pool's ready queue, and the next free worker picks it up. No coordinator.

The dispatch layer is **code that lives on the worker, not a separate role**. The worker IS the dispatcher when it's running a task.

## Same-language fast path

Yes — let's bake this in as a spec contract. Each spec exposes two invoke paths:

```c
typedef struct {
    const char *name;
    const char *file_ext;

    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);
    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
    int   (*compile)(...);

    // Universal path — language-agnostic JSON bytes in/out
    int   (*invoke_json)(void *handle, ..., const void **input_data,
                         const int *input_sizes, int n_inputs,
                         void *out_buf, int out_buf_capacity, int *out_size);

    // Fast path — bytes are this language's own serialization
    int   (*invoke_native)(void *handle, ..., /* same shape, different bytes */);

    // Optional callbacks the spec uses to convert between native and JSON
    // forms when a wire crosses language boundaries
    int   (*native_to_json)(const void *src, int src_size,
                            void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
    int   (*json_to_native)(const void *src, int src_size,
                            void *dst, int dst_capacity, int *dst_size);
} lang_spec_t;
```

**Compile-time check:** the build step (issue 309) walks every wire. For each, look up producer's spec and consumer's spec. If they're the same language, mark the wire as `fast_path: true`. If different, `fast_path: false`. The wire flag is part of the compiled manifest.

**Runtime:** the dispatch action checks the wire's flag when reading an input:
- `fast_path: true` → bytes in the slot are already in the consumer's native serialization. Pass directly to `invoke_native`.
- `fast_path: false` → bytes are JSON. Pass to `invoke_json`, which decodes JSON into native types.

**Producer side:** if a box's output goes only to same-language consumers, the producer writes native bytes. If it goes to mixed consumers, the producer writes JSON (the universal format) and consumers convert as needed via `json_to_native`. If the producer wants to optimize the same-language case while still serving cross-language consumers, the dispatch layer can detect the mixed scenario and ask the producer for both — but that's a refinement.

Simpler default: **producer writes one format; the dispatch layer decides which based on whether ALL consumers are the same language.**

| Producer lang | All consumers same lang? | Bytes in slot |
|---------------|--------------------------|---------------|
| Lua           | yes (all Lua)            | Lua-native    |
| Lua           | no (mixed)               | JSON          |
| C             | yes (all C)              | C struct memcpy |
| C             | no                       | JSON          |
| Bash          | any                      | strings (no native gain) |

**Where the boundary check happens:** at compile time only. The wire flag is set once and never changes at runtime. The dispatch action just reads the flag.

**Compiling same-language boxes into one file:** that's a deeper optimization — whole-program compilation per language. The C spec could merge all C boxes into one translation unit, which lets the compiler inline across box boundaries. The Lua spec could pre-compile all Lua functions into one bytecode chunk loaded into one shared `lua_State`. Both would let the runtime skip not just JSON but also some of the function-call overhead. This is a phase-3.5-or-later optimization; the wire-level fast path described above is the immediate win.

## Assistant

Now your other questions:

## Why iterators have ring buffers and non-iterators don't

The difference comes from the **task lifecycle**, not from any property of the values being passed.

A non-iterator box's task lives once. It runs, produces one slot full of bytes, fires its outgoing connections, and dies. Each consumer downstream got spawned as its own task with that producer's slot in its inputs. The slot only ever needs to hold **one** value because the producer only ever produces one. Multiple consumers of the same producer all peek into that same one cell.

An iterator's task lives **across many invocations**. Its input slot has to persist long enough for many upstream pushes — the iterator pops one, processes, re-spawns itself, the new spawn pops the next. Because the upstream may have already produced the next value (or several) before the iterator gets to consume them, the slot needs **N cells** to hold the backlog.

If you tried to give a non-iterator slot a ring buffer, you'd be modeling something that can't happen: a single-task producer can't produce multiple values because it only runs once. The "fast producer / slow consumer" you described isn't fast-multiple-pushes-into-one-slot — it's many-task-instances-with-many-slots, queued through the **pool's task queue**, not through a slot ring buffer. The pool's queue is the rate buffer for plain wires; the slot's ring buffer is the rate buffer for iterators specifically.

## How language specs handle bytes/JSON

The dispatch action hands `invoke` a list of `(byte_pointer, byte_size)` pairs. The spec's job is the bridge between bytes and language-native types. **Convention is JSON** as the lowest-common-denominator encoding because every shipped language has a JSON parser and JSON's type tags carry the right shape across language boundaries.

For a Lua box receiving a wire value:
1. Spec's `invoke` is given `input_data[0] = "\"hello\""`, `input_sizes[0] = 7`.
2. Spec parses those bytes (cjson decode) into a Lua value pushed on the Lua stack.
3. Calls the user function via `lua_pcall`.
4. Reads the return from the Lua stack.
5. Encodes back to JSON bytes, writes them to `out_buf`, sets `*out_size`.

The slot store never sees what's IN the bytes. The bytes are opaque to it — they could be JSON, msgpack, raw struct memcpy, or hieroglyphs. Whatever the producer's spec wrote is what the consumer's spec gets, byte-for-byte.

The fast-path issue (312) lets two specs of the same language agree to use a non-JSON encoding for their slots, skipping the encode/decode cost on same-language wires. Different-language wires still go through JSON because that's the only thing both endpoints can speak.

## High-performance is the bar, agreed

Then the same-language fast path (312) goes from "future optimization" to "default behavior." A user wiring two Lua boxes together pays no JSON cost; a user wiring Lua to C pays one encode + one decode (unavoidable cross-language tax).

The whole-program same-language merge mentioned at the bottom of 312 — where the C spec compiles every C box into one translation unit and every box-boundary becomes a regular function call with no slot-store involvement at all — is the right end state for tight inner loops. It's a bigger lift but worth tracking. I'll fold it into 312 as the explicit phase-3.5 next step rather than just an offhand mention.

Want me to also open a follow-on for **type-annotated wire encodings** — primitives go raw bytes, strings as length-prefixed UTF-8, complex types as JSON — so the high-throughput numeric case has a path even across language boundaries? That's where the performance ceiling actually lifts; same-language fast-path is necessary but not sufficient.

## Assistant

Now your questions answered, with the architectural updates I just made:

## A box called 30 or 50 times

The fix is in 302/304 just now: I rewrote the slot model to **slots belong to input ports, not tasks**. Each box has one slot per input port, allocated at graph load and persisting for the run. Tasks are ephemeral — each one pops one value from each input slot, runs, and disappears. A box invoked 30 times is 30 task instances over the same 30 inputs flowing through the same set of port slots.

So my earlier "non-iterators run once" was wrong. Non-iterators run **as many times as their inputs supply values**. The iterator-vs-non-iterator distinction collapses at the slot level: both have queued input slots, both spawn dynamically.

## Where slots live before producers fire

On the consumer's box, in its per-port slot table. Allocated at load time, empty until something gets pushed in. The wait is at the **dispatch layer's spawn-check**, not on a slot's wait list — when no input has been pushed yet, the spawn rule simply doesn't fire. The slot exists, ready to receive.

## How the dispatch layer creates new tasks

It allocates a `dispatch_task_t` from the pool's task allocator and submits it. The task struct is small (`box_id` + counter snapshot) — slots aren't on it because they're durable per-box state. When phase 5 (push to downstream input slots) of a producer's task completes, each pushed-to slot's owning box checks the spawn rule; if all input slots now hold a value, the dispatch layer allocates a fresh task for that box and queues it.

## Iterators: simpler, no self-respawn

You called this exactly. With per-port queued slots, an iterator no longer needs to re-spawn itself. The dispatch layer's spawn-on-input-arrival rule handles re-invocation automatically — every push to the iterator's input slot triggers a spawn-check. The iterator's counter is per-box state, not per-task, so each new task just reads the box's current counter, routes its output to that branch, and increments. After 5 inputs there are 5 task instances, after the 6th there's a 6th — counter just keeps incrementing mod N.

## "Box exists at runtime" — yes

A `box_runtime_state` struct per box holds the counter, the input slot table, the language spec handle, the producer set (for end-of-stream detection). Tasks are stack frames on workers; boxes are durable structs. Your sketch was right: each box has its input slots, and the dispatch layer pops + spawns when inputs are ready.

## Cross-iterator pairing

Two iterators A and B feeding a non-iterator C: C has its own per-port queue, A pushes into one queue and B pushes into the other, C spawns tasks FIFO — each task pops one from each queue. If A and B push in deterministic order, pairing is `(A_i, B_i)`. If they push concurrently from different workers, pairing depends on race winners.

For phase 3 the policy is **FIFO** with an explicit note that workloads needing strict-index pairing get per-invocation index tags on values. Added that note to 304.

## Same-language merge — opened as 313

Research-only, low priority, blocked until rest of phase 3 ships. The deliverable is a written design — not code — answering: how do iterators interact with merging, what's the compile-time hit, how do stack traces work. Recommendation comes out of the research, not before.

## 312 note

Added: implementer must ask the user about type-annotated cross-language encodings (primitive numbers as raw bytes vs as JSON strings) before coding the fast path. The decision affects the spec interface shape, so it can't be retrofitted cheaply.

## What changed in the issue files

- **302**: slot model rewritten. Per-port slots replace per-task slots. Refcounting replaced with producer-set tracking. Spawning is "all inputs available" trigger, not "task submitted then blocks."
- **304**: task struct simplified (no input_slots, no output_slot — those are on the box). Phase 1 (check inputs) deleted (input availability is the spawn precondition). Iterator self-respawn removed. Cross-iterator pairing section added.
- **312**: implementation-time note added.
- **313**: new, research/blocked.
