## Assistant

Walkthrough of the design as it stands. Let me go layer by layer.

## 1. The big picture

SoraMech is split into three programs:
- **Editor** (web, JS): visual graph editor that writes `maps/<name>/` directory contents.
- **Runner** (C, phase 3): pool runner that executes a map.
- **Server** (Lua, phase 2 → eventually disposable): file CRUD between editor and disk.

The map directory is the contract between editor and runner. Boxes live in `maps/<name>/boxes/*.json`; source files in `maps/<name>/src/`; meta in `maps/<name>/meta.json`. The editor writes and the runner reads.

## 2. The graph

A **box** is a node. Two kinds:
- `call`: invokes a user-written function in some language.
- `data`: language-agnostic file source / sink (issue 229, planned — currently behaves like a colored call box).

A **port** is an input or output position on a box. Inputs are named (from the parsed function signature). Outputs are either a single unnamed port, or three named branches `lt`/`eq`/`gt` (comparator), or N user-named slots (iterator).

A **wire** is a routing declaration: producer's named output port → consumer's named input port. Wires don't own data; they're metadata in the graph.

Three **box modes**, all set per-box rather than per-kind:
- **Plain call**: function runs, returns one value, value goes to all wired downstream consumers unconditionally.
- **Comparator**: same, plus the value is compared to the box's `comparand` field; only the matching branch fires.
- **Iterator**: no function. Per invocation, the box copies its input to one of N output ports based on a counter, then increments. Pure routing.

## 3. Startup (issue 301)

The pool runner's `main`:
1. Parse map path from argv.
2. Load + validate the graph (C loader, issue 305 — replaces phase 2's `003-loader.lua`).
3. For each box: allocate one ring-buffer slot per input port. Sizes from compile-time analysis (1 for non-iterator-fed, larger for iterator-fed, growable on demand).
4. Build per-box runtime state: `box_runtime_state[box_id]` holding the input slot table, the iterator counter (0), the producer set per slot (boxes that may still push there).
5. Create the worker pool (3d-rts vendored library, `libs/task-pool/`). Each worker calls each language spec's `init(worker_idx)` to stand up its per-thread state — one `lua_State`, one persistent Bash subprocess, one C dlopen table per worker.
6. Push every input port's literal value (if set) directly into its slot.
7. For each box whose input set is now satisfied (every input slot has a value), spawn a `dispatch_task_t` and submit to the pool.
8. Main thread sleeps on a condition variable waiting for the active-task counter to hit zero.
9. On wake, write `last-run.jsonl`, tear down workers, exit.

After step 8, **no main-thread orchestration**. Workers drive everything.

## 4. Slots (issue 302, revised)

Each box owns one ring-buffer slot per input port. Slot header is 20 bytes:

```
cell_capacity (4) | n_cells (4) | head (4) | tail (4) | touched (1) | _pad (3)
followed by n_cells * (4-byte size + cell_capacity bytes) of cell data
```

Pushes write a cell + advance `tail`; pops read at `head` + advance `head`. `touched` is a per-slot spinlock byte.

**Allocation strategy**: size-class free lists. Compile time enumerates every distinct slot size; lists pre-populate at startup; allocations are list pops (O(1)). Variable-size payloads (LLM responses, dynamic arrays) live in a separate **large-value heap**; the slot stores a `{size, offset}` handle into it. When a slot's pre-allocated `n_cells` proves too small, the slot grows (lists grow during the run).

**Lifetime**: durable for the run. No per-slot refcounting. End-of-stream is detected via the **producer set** per slot: the dispatch layer tracks which producer boxes may still push, and removes producers as they finish. When the set is empty AND the slot is drained, the consumer has end-of-stream on that port.

## 5. Dispatch (issue 304, revised)

A **task** is `{ box_id, counter }`. That's it. Slots aren't on the task because they're durable on the box.

**Spawning rule** (the synchronization primitive of the whole system):
> When a push to box B's input slot completes, check: does every input slot on B now hold at least one value? If yes, allocate a task with `box_id=B, counter=B.counter`, submit to the pool.

**The dispatch action** (`dispatch_action(task)`), runs on whichever worker pulled the task:

1. **Read inputs**: `slot_pop` from each of B's input slots into heap buffers. Pop drains; values queued behind move forward.
2. **Branch on box mode**:
   - Plain call → invoke spec, write return to outputs.
   - Comparator → invoke spec, write return to outputs, compare return to `comparand`, fire only matching branch.
   - Iterator → no spec, route popped value to `iterator_outputs[counter]`, increment counter mod N.
3. **Push to downstream input slots**: the box's connection list has the wire targets. For each wire that should fire (per mode), `slot_push(downstream_input_slot, bytes, size)`. After each push, run the spawn-rule check for that downstream box — possibly spawn another task.
4. **End-of-stream propagation**: if this push was the producer's last expected push, remove this producer from each pushed-to slot's producer set. Empty set + drained queue = consumer's end-of-stream on that port.
5. Return ACT_DONE. Pool decrements active-task counter. If counter hits zero, signal main thread.

No phase blocks. No wait lists. Tasks are spawn-then-run-to-completion.

**Cross-iterator pairing**: when a non-iterator C has two upstream iterators A and B, each port has its own queue, FIFO pop pairs `(A_i, B_i)` deterministically iff A and B push in deterministic order. Concurrent pushes from different workers risk race. Phase 3 ships FIFO; per-invocation index tags are a follow-on if a workload needs strict pairing.

## 6. Language specs (issue 303 + 312)

Each language ships `langs/<name>/spec.so` and `langs/<name>/lexer.js` and `langs/<name>/parser.js` (issue 231, planned).

Spec interface (with the 312 fast-path additions):

```c
typedef struct {
    const char *name, *file_ext;

    void *(*init)(int worker_idx);
    void  (*teardown)(void *handle);
    int   (*compile)(const char *src_path, const char *out_path, const box_t *box);

    int   (*invoke_json)  (handle, file, fn, in_data[], in_sizes[], n, out_buf, out_cap, out_size);
    int   (*invoke_native)(handle, file, fn, in_data[], in_sizes[], n, out_buf, out_cap, out_size);

    int   (*native_to_json)(src, src_size, dst, dst_cap, dst_size);
    int   (*json_to_native)(src, src_size, dst, dst_cap, dst_size);
} lang_spec_t;
```

The pool runner `dlopen`s every `langs/<name>/spec.so` at startup and indexes by name and extension.

**Wire format** is JSON by default — every spec implements `invoke_json` for cross-language hops. Same-language wires use `invoke_native` with a per-language native serialization (Lua tables via msgpack or `lua_dump`; C structs via memcpy; Bash strings as-is). The compile step (issue 309) classifies each wire as `fast_path: true | false` based on whether endpoints share a language, and tags the producer's output_format. Dispatch action's input-read branches on this flag — no runtime negotiation.

**Type-annotated cross-language encodings** (primitives as raw bytes, strings length-prefixed, structures as JSON) — open question per 312's implementation note. Decision deferred to implementation start.

**Per-language specs**:
- Lua (306): `lua_State` per worker, file caching via Lua registry, in-process invocation.
- C (307): `dlopen`/`dlsym` per worker, compile callback generates a per-box wrapper that takes a count + pointer array and forwards to the user function.
- Bash (308): persistent subprocess per worker, length-prefixed message protocol over Unix domain socket. Out-of-process because Bash has no embeddable runtime.

## 7. Build / compile (issue 309)

Top-level Makefile orchestrates. Targets:
- `runner` → `soramech-pool` binary, statically linking the pool, slot store, graph loader, dispatch, spec registry.
- `specs` → recurses into each `langs/<name>/Makefile` to build `spec.so`.
- `clean`, `test`, `DEBUG=1`, `STRICT=1` modes.

**Compile-and-package step** (folded in from issue 219, closed): turns an editable map directory into `maps/<name>/compiled/` with the runner binary, source files, per-box `.so`s, language spec `.so`s, and a manifest. Triggered by the editor's Compile button (issue 222) via a `POST /maps/<name>/compile` endpoint and by a CLI `soramech-compile <map-dir>`.

**Compile-time validations** (also during compile):
- Required-input rule (issue 230): every input port has a wire, a literal, or `optional: true`. Compile fails loudly if not.
- Wire fast-path classification (issue 312): each wire tagged `fast_path` per same/different language pair.
- Producer-set determination per input slot for end-of-stream tracking.
- Output sizes per box for the slot-store size-class allocator.

## 8. Quiescence and run output

Each task spawn increments the active-task counter; each ACT_DONE decrements. When the counter hits zero, no task is running and no further spawns are pending — run is over. Iterators that hit end-of-stream stop spawning successors naturally; the counter drains.

**No deadlock concern** at runtime. The graph loader rejects non-iterator cycles and inputs without producers at load time. Spec errors abort the program (no halfway-complete state). Iterator-parked-on-empty-input is the natural EOS condition, not a deadlock.

`last-run.jsonl` is written at the end (issue 311): one JSON line per box invocation with timing, inputs, outputs, errors. Replay-friendly format.

## 9. Cross-cutting concerns

- **No null from missing inputs** (issue 230): compile fails on unwired non-optional ports; absent ≠ explicit null at the spec boundary.
- **Variadic** (per-box flag, issue 217 complete; issue 231 planned for per-language parser): user-tagged in editor, dispatch flattens slots into ordered arg list, language's native variadic syntax handles it.
- **Self-loop wires** (issue 228 complete): allowed; dispatch just pushes from a box's output to its own input slot like any other push.
- **Optional inputs** (issue 230): port's `optional: true` flag; spawn rule allows the task even with that slot empty; the spec's invocation passes a shorter arg list.

## 10. Editor surface to runtime

Things in the editor that produce runtime state:
- `kind`, `ref`, `fn` → which box, which language, which function.
- `inputs[].name`, `inputs[].value`, `inputs[].optional` → input port names, literals, optionality.
- `comparand` → comparator threshold.
- `iterator_outputs[]` → iterator branch names + count.
- `variadic_inputs[]` → which input names are variadic group bases.
- `connections[]` → wires.
- `has_output` → sink box flag.

The runtime never reads anything outside this set from the box JSON. The editor is the source of truth for graph shape; the runner is the source of truth for execution.

---

That's the design end-to-end. Where do you want to dig deeper, or what doesn't sit right?

## Assistant

Good catches — several of these point at design holes. Let me work through them, then we update the issues.

## 1. Why iterator-fed ports get larger buffers

It's backlog absorption. A non-iterator producer pushes its slot **once** for its lifetime; one cell is enough. An iterator produces N values across N invocations; if N is 50 and the consumer can only drain at half the producer's rate, the slot has to hold up to 25 unconsumed values at any moment.

For non-iterator-fed ports, the producer-rate question doesn't arise — there's only ever one push.

## 2. Owning the thread pool, not vendoring 3d-rts

Agreed. The pool is part of SoraMech's surface area; we want full control over the spawn primitive, the worker-context model, and any extensions (tying iterators to specific workers, see §8 below). We'll write a SoraMech-specific pool and refer to 3d-rts only as the design reference / inspiration. Worth updating issue 301.

## 3. Init synchronization

Right — we need a barrier after every worker has called every spec's `init` and before any task is submitted. Otherwise the main thread could push tasks while a worker is still bringing up its `lua_State`, and a task that lands on the not-yet-initialized worker would read a null handle.

The shape: pool startup is a join point. Workers come up, each one runs all the spec inits in some defined order, and signals readiness on a counter. Main thread waits until all N workers have signaled, **then** pushes literals and spawns initial tasks. Worth updating 301.

## 4. The literal re-push problem (you found a real hole)

Right. With the pop-draining model I wrote, a literal pushed once at startup gets consumed by the first task and the second task finds an empty slot. Broken for any box that runs more than once.

The fix is to bring back a distinction I'd flattened: **non-iterator-fed slots are peek-not-pop, iterator-fed slots are pop-and-drain**. Original 302 had this; I lost it in the rewrite.

Concretely:
- A 1-cell slot's read mode is **peek**: the value stays after a read, every subsequent task on this box reads the same value.
- An N-cell slot's read mode is **pop**: each read drains one cell, subsequent tasks see the next value.

For the user's question: a literal pushed at startup stays in the slot. If the box runs 50 times because some other input port (iterator-fed, N-cell, drained) supplies 50 values, each of those 50 task instances peeks the literal. The literal isn't re-pushed — it doesn't need to be.

The spawn-rule check becomes: "every input port's slot has at least one value **available**" — which means "non-empty cell" for both kinds, just with different semantics on read.

This deserves an update to 302, both the slot-read section and the spawn-rule section.

## 5. End-of-stream / producer set — you're right, drop it

Your read is exactly right: the producer-set machinery is overengineering. The active-task counter (issue 301) already handles run termination correctly:
- Each task spawn increments.
- Each ACT_DONE decrements.
- When it hits zero with no pending spawns, the run is over.

The producer-set was answering "should this iterator stop expecting more inputs?" — but iterators don't need to ask that question. They just stop getting tasks when their input slot stops getting pushed. The active-task counter naturally drains, the run ends.

I shouldn't have added the producer-set thing. Let me strip it from 302/304. The end-of-stream condition becomes trivial: there is no end-of-stream condition, just an active-task counter reaching zero.

Box JSON has the producer→consumer relationships; runtime doesn't need to mutate that. You're right that mutating-the-graph-at-runtime felt like garbage collection — it was, and the cost wasn't worth it.

## 6. Large value passed C → Lua

Honest answer: **cross-language large values get copied at the boundary, no way around it**.

Lua's string memory is owned by Lua's GC. C-allocated memory has no relationship to Lua's string table. There's no zero-copy path: even Lua's userdata, which can wrap a C pointer, requires Lua code to call back into C to read bytes — defeating the purpose if you're trying to pass it to a Lua function expecting a string.

The flow:
1. C spec produces 4MB string. Writes it to the large-value heap. Slot cell holds `{size: 4MB, offset: ...}`.
2. Lua spec invoked. Receives the 16-byte handle. Follows the offset, finds the bytes in the large-value heap.
3. **Copies** those bytes into a Lua string via `lua_pushlstring(L, bytes, 4MB)`. Lua's GC now owns a copy.
4. The user's Lua function gets a normal Lua string.

The 4MB copy is the price of going across the language boundary. Lua's memory model is incompatible with referencing external memory.

Same-language paths can avoid the copy: C → C can pass the handle directly; the consumer's wrapper reads from the same large-value heap with no copy. Lua → Lua can reuse Lua's own table-passing via `lua_dump`/`lua_load` or msgpack. That's what the same-language fast path (issue 312) buys you.

You're right that this is "an impossible optimization" for cross-language. The right move isn't to optimize the cross-language case — it's to design graphs so large values don't cross language boundaries. The same-language merge research issue (313) eventually lets the build system warn about this kind of crossing.

## 7. Counter as invisible input port

Both work. The choice is between:

**A: Per-box counter state** (current 304). Box has `counter` field. Each spawn snapshots `counter` into the task; box's counter increments. Pro: simple, one int per iterator box, no extra slot machinery. Con: special-cases iterator boxes in the runtime.

**B: Counter as a hidden input slot.** Box has a 1-cell slot named `__counter__` (invisible to user). At spawn time, the dispatch layer pops the counter value, runs the action, pushes counter+1 back to the slot. Pro: counter goes through the same machinery as everything else; no special box state. Con: more pushes/pops on the hot path; the slot is conceptually special even if structurally uniform.

I'd lean A for performance; B is more elegant. Worth picking explicitly — let me know which way and I'll lock it in 304.

## 8. Cross-iterator pairing under "phase 3 as designed"

OK, let's commit to a pairing answer.

The race you're worried about: iterator A produces 50 invocations. Task 1 produces value `a0`, task 2 produces `a1`, etc. Tasks could run on different workers in parallel; if task 2 finishes before task 1, the pushes to consumer C's slot arrive in the wrong order.

But — iterator tasks share the **per-box counter** as state. To spawn task 2, the dispatch layer reads the counter, snapshots it, then increments. Two iterator tasks of the same iterator can't really run in parallel anyway: they have a serial dependency through the counter.

The cleanest answer is **iterators are pinned to a single worker**. Each iterator has its tasks executed sequentially on that worker. Push order = invocation order = FIFO arrival at the consumer's slot, deterministic.

Trade-off: an iterator's invocations don't parallelize across workers. Other tasks (downstream consumers, parallel iterators on different workers) absolutely do. Most graphs aren't bottlenecked on a single iterator's throughput; they're bottlenecked on the consumers' work.

For cases where one iterator IS the bottleneck and parallel invocations would help — that's the "indexed pairing" follow-on. Each push tags its value with the iterator's counter snapshot; consumer's slot becomes a priority-insert structure that pops in tag order. Strict ordering, parallel iterator invocations. Complex enough that we don't ship it without proof we need it.

So phase 3 as designed: **single-worker iterators + FIFO consumer slots, deterministic pairing as a free consequence**.

Right, you're correcting valid mistakes in my framing. Let me work through the answers, then make the updates.

## 1. Box vs task clarification

You're right, my terminology was sloppy. To re-state precisely:

**Boxes** are graph nodes — static, defined in JSON, exist for the run. **Tasks** are box invocations — ephemeral, allocated when a spawn fires. A box may have 1 or N tasks during a run.

What I meant by "iterator producer pushes 50 times": *the iterator box, across its 50 task invocations, performs 50 pushes total.* Each individual iterator task does the same single push that any task does. The box is what fires multiple times; each task does exactly one thing.

You're also right that "iterator vs non-iterator" isn't really the buffer-size axis. **Any box can have many tasks**. A non-iterator downstream of an iterator runs as many times as the iterator fires its branch. Its output gets pushed downstream that many times.

So buffer-size analysis is really: "how many task invocations of the producer box, and how many of the consumer box, and what's the worst-case backlog at the slot." Iterator-fed is the most common case where N>1, but not the only one. I'll restate 302 to use the "box invocation count" framing.

## 2. 1-cell vs N-cell

`n_cells` is the number of cells in the ring buffer — i.e., how many distinct values the slot can hold simultaneously. `cell_capacity` is the size in bytes of a single cell. A 1-cell slot holds one value (single-value); an N-cell slot is a queue with N positions.

The *read mode* (peek vs pop) was historically tied to capacity in the design — 1-cell peek, N-cell pop. They're orthogonal in principle, but coupling them simplifies the API: if you need to keep a value around, allocate 1-cell + peek; if you need a queue, allocate N-cell + pop.

## 3. Counter approach + functional alternative

Functional alternative I considered: an iterator is a regular box with two inputs — `value` and `counter` — where `counter` is fed by a separate "counter generator" box. The counter generator self-loops: takes its previous output as input, emits previous+1. The iterator routes `value` to `outputs[counter % N]`. No iterator-internal state, fully data-driven.

Why I'd still go with per-box counter for our case: the functional version **serializes counter generation** through the counter box's tasks. Each counter task depends on the previous via the self-loop. To parallelize iterator invocations you'd need the counter box to pre-emit faster than consumers drain, which essentially requires a separate scheduling discipline for the counter box.

The per-box-counter-with-atomic-increment-at-spawn version gets parallelism for free: at task spawn, atomic fetch-and-add on the counter, snapshot into the task. Sibling iterator tasks run on different workers with independent counter values. Counter is a single int + atomic op per spawn.

So I'd ship per-box counter with atomic spawn-time increment, but I'll note the functional alternative in 304 as a documented trade-off for future reconsideration if it ever bites.

## 4. Parallel iterator execution — yes, achievable

With atomic spawn-time counter increment, iterator tasks can run in parallel. The race that's left is **push ordering at the consumer**: if iterator A's tasks 5 and 6 run on different workers, task 6 might push to consumer C first.

Solution: **each push carries a tag** equal to the source iterator's counter at task-spawn time. Consumer slots become tag-ordered (priority queue): pop returns the lowest-tag value, not the head value. For non-iterator producers, tag is always 0 (or omitted); FIFO falls out for free.

Cost: ~4-8 bytes per cell for the tag field. Slot pop becomes O(log n) instead of O(1) for tag-ordered slots. Compile-time analysis tags only the slots that need ordering (downstream of iterators, in branches that preserve order).

This restores deterministic pairing and lets iterator tasks parallelize. It's the "indexed pairing" follow-on, but for "phase 3 as designed" we should pull it forward. Worth specifying now, not later.

Let me work through both threads.

## Self-push counter

The design works:

```
Iterator box (call type)
  Inputs:
    value    (from upstream, N-cell pop slot)
    counter  (self-loop, N-cell pop slot, primed with 0 at startup)
  Outputs (N branches)
    output_0, output_1, ... output_{N-1}
    next_counter (self-loop wire back to counter input)

Per invocation:
  pop value, pop counter
  route value to outputs[counter % N]
  push (counter + 1) to next_counter
```

Counter is data, not box state. Conceptually clean — the iterator becomes a regular call box that happens to self-loop one of its outputs.

**The catch you already identified**: tasks serialize through the counter dependency. Task K can't spawn until task K-1 has pushed counter=K. Even though we accept non-deterministic routing, the dispatch chain is still single-file because no task has its counter input ready until the previous one finishes. So we lose the parallelism the atomic-fetch-add design buys.

You said "we make no guarantees that inputs will route to output ports in a deterministic way" — that's about *which* branch a value goes to, not about whether tasks run in parallel. Even with ordering-relaxed semantics, the counter input being a single-stream queue forces serial execution.

To get parallelism with the self-push design, the counter input would need to be **pre-populated** with multiple values upfront (so task 0, 1, 2 can all spawn immediately). That requires knowing N at compile time, which we don't.

So: self-push is the cleaner design conceptually, atomic-fetch-add is the better design for performance. Given high-performance is a stated goal, I'd stick with atomic-fetch-add. Functional purity isn't worth losing core-scaling.

## Merging iterator and comparator

There are three levels at which they could merge. Worth picking explicitly.

### Level A — Mental-model unification only

Both are "branching boxes" in the editor and docs. Same conceptual category: "this box has multiple output branches, only one fires per invocation, the box's settings decide which." Comparator and iterator are two presets under that umbrella. Editor UI groups them. Runtime keeps them as separate dispatch-action paths.

Cost: zero code change.
Win: clearer narrative for users learning the editor.

### Level B — Unified router schema, separate dispatch paths

A box has a `routing` field:

```json
{ "routing": { "kind": "comparator", "comparand": 4 } }
{ "routing": { "kind": "iterator",   "outputs": ["a","b","c"] } }
```

Both kinds carry their own params; the dispatch layer reads `routing.kind` and branches into the appropriate code path. The schema is unified; the runtime isn't really.

Cost: small refactor (current `comparand` and `iterator_outputs` move under `routing`). Editor UI consolidates.
Win: one place to add routing kinds (e.g., user-defined router via spec callback), one place for compile validation.

### Level C — One unified dispatch path

A "router" abstraction at the dispatch layer:

```
Dispatch action for a router box:
  1. (optional) Invoke spec on inputs → returns value (and possibly a branch hint)
  2. Compute branch via routing_rule:
       - "comparand"        → compare(value, params.comparand) → lt/eq/gt
       - "counter"          → params.counter++ % N → output_K
       - "function_return"  → spec returned (value, branch); use branch
       - "fan_all"          → no branch, fire all (= plain call)
  3. Push value (or popped input, for counter-mode without spec) to selected branch(es)
```

Box modes become routing-rule values:
- Plain call → `"fan_all"`
- Comparator → spec runs + `"comparand"` rule
- Iterator → no spec + `"counter"` rule
- (New) Function-decided routing → spec runs + `"function_return"` rule

Cost: real refactor. Dispatch-action code consolidated. Spec interface grows a way for `invoke` to return a branch tag (already on the table for "function-defined routing" anyway).
Win: future routing kinds slot in cleanly. Iterator-with-function (your "functions iterating their own outputs") becomes natural — just a router with both spec and counter rule, or with `function_return` rule.

### Recommendation

I'd ship **Level B now**, queue **Level C** for after phase 3 ships. Level B is a small refactor, makes the schema coherent, and sets up Level C cleanly. Level C is the right end-state but requires settling the spec-returns-branch contract, which is a bigger commit.

Level A happens for free as part of B (the doc reframes naturally).

Want me to:
1. Open an issue for Level B (the schema unification)?
2. Add Level C as a planned-but-deferred follow-on?

Or pick a different path through this?

## Atomic-counter slot — yes, this works

A new slot mode that's just "an atomic int with a special read op":

```c
// Allocated with SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER flag
slot_alloc(store, /*cell_capacity*/ 4, /*n_cells*/ 1, SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER);

// Read-and-increment, atomic
int slot_read_inc(store, slot_id, int mod);
// equivalent to:
//   uint32_t v = atomic_fetch_add(&slot->cell[0].data, 1);
//   return v % mod;
```

From the outside: a regular slot. Consumers see a slot value that moves; they don't see the box state. Concurrent readers each get a unique value because of the atomic fetch-add. No push needed; the read is the increment.

The iterator box's dispatch action becomes:
```
1. Pop value from value-input slot.
2. branch_idx = slot_read_inc(counter_slot, n_outputs);
3. Push value to outputs[branch_idx], with tag=branch_idx
4. Done.
```

Counter lives in a slot, not in `box_runtime_state`. Iterators don't have any per-box mutable state — they're just a routing rule plus a designated atomic-counter slot. Parallel iterator tasks all read from the same atomic slot independently; each gets a unique value. Counter overflow wraps mod N gracefully.

The slot store knows about this read mode (it owns the atomic primitive); the dispatch layer just calls `slot_read_inc`. The spec never sees any of it.

This unifies nicely: atomic-counter slots are a building block that any feature needing "thread-safe sequence" can use, not just iterators.

## Assistant

Done. Summary:

**Atomic-counter slot** — added to 302 as `SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER` mode + `slot_read_inc(slot, mod)` op. The slot's "cell" is just an atomic uint32 under the hood; the read op atomically fetches-and-adds, returns mod N. Iterator counter moves into this slot; box state loses its `counter` field. Iterators now parallel by default — concurrent `slot_read_inc` calls give each task a unique routing index.

**304** updated to read counter via `slot_read_inc` mid-action rather than at spawn. Task struct shrinks to just `{ box_id }` — no per-task counter snapshot needed because the slot itself is the source of truth.

**Issue 233** opened for Level B: unified `routing: { kind, ... }` field replacing separate `comparand` and `iterator_outputs`. Includes:
- Schema migration plan for legacy maps (loader-side, transparent).
- Inspector `mode` dropdown replacing the two separate toggles.
- An open question about function-backed iterators (recommendation: support; the dispatch flow naturally accommodates "function runs, output gets routed" for both comparator and iterator).
- Level C explicitly noted as deferred follow-on.

Spec interface stays clean — the spec sees only `invoke(bytes_in) → bytes_out`, no awareness of routing or branches. Routing decisions are dispatch-layer concerns; counter values are slot-store concerns. The user-facing concept of "branching boxes" is editor + map-file knowledge.

Where do you want to go next? Implementation order of phase 2 issues we've opened (229, 230, 231, 233), or another design thread to chase?

## Assistant

Done. Summary of what your annotations changed in 233:

**Iterator runs its function** — no more "iterator passes input through unchanged" option. Every branching box runs its function; the routing kind only picks where the function's output goes. The ticket reads this as the canonical single behavior, not as one of two options.

**Dropped speculative kinds** — `function_decided` and `user_router` are gone. Both leaked language-spec or function-author concerns into the routing layer; they violate "routing logic is dispatch + editor only, never spec."

**New kinds added per your brainstorm**:
- `randomizer` — hash the atomic counter, mod N. Spread distribution from a deterministic-given-seed counter.
- `weighted` — user-defined percentages per branch (e.g., `[0.8, 0.2]`); compile-time normalized to a cumulative band table; lookup against the counter scaled to a 1000-step precision.
- `distributor` — argmin over downstream slot fill levels. Sends to the least-busy branch. Ties fall back to round-robin.

All three reuse the same `SLOT_ATOMIC_COUNTER` machinery introduced in 302 — they're variations on "read the counter, do something kind-specific."

**Multi-band comparator** captured as a brainstorm section (your range example: `< 3`, `3-7`, `7-11`, `11-17`, `> 17`). Defers schema specifics; ships single-threshold first; multi-band lands once a real use case demands it.

**Migration simplified** — manual hand-edit of the existing maps; no loader-side legacy adapter. The schema validator can outright reject the old `comparand` / `iterator_outputs` shapes once the unified field ships.

**Inspector port-naming** — comparator stays `lt/eq/gt`, iterator/randomizer/weighted/distributor use `out_0..out_N-1` conventions; ports are not renameable per the editor's existing rule (issue 224).

**Level C example** — replaced the vague "deferred follow-on" with a concrete sketch: a `routing.kind: "rules"` with a list of `{ when: <expr>, to: <branch> }` evaluated by a small in-dispatch expression evaluator. Every existing kind reduces to a "rules" expression. Ships when Level B is in production and we have real maps showing the consolidation pays off.

**304** updated to match: dispatch action runs the function for every call box; routing-kind table replaces the old plain/comparator/iterator three-mode framing; phase 3 (the old "skip invoke for iterators" branch) is gone — there's just one uniform path with a kind-specific branch picker in phase 5.

Want to start implementing 233 now, or polish more of its open questions first (multi-band edges, distributor under fan-out, weighted ties)?

## Assistant

Done. Summary of the latest annotation pass:

**Branch-level fan-out** — added a section clarifying that fan-out works on a per-branch basis. If the comparator's `lt` port has three wires, then each time the routing decision picks `lt`, all three wires fire (a copy is pushed into each consumer's slot). On the next invocation if `eq` is picked, only `eq`'s wires fire. Each branch port acts as its own little plain-output port when activated.

**Always-explicit `routing` field** — removed the "absence means plain" default. Plain boxes carry `routing: { "kind": "plain" }` exactly the same way branched boxes carry `routing: { "kind": "comparator", ... }`. Schema rejects boxes without a routing field. New `kind="plain"` section added.

**Doubled-threshold equality** — folded your encoding into the multi-band comparator brainstorm. `thresholds: [3, 3]` means "less than 3, exactly 3, greater than 3" via a zero-width point band. This makes the legacy lt/eq/gt exactly `thresholds: [c, c]` — the editor can present a single-comparand UI but store the doubled form, so the dispatch layer has one consistent representation.

**Weighted UI** — captured your slider+knob design with a per-port line: visual band in the middle, output port name on the left, percentage on the right. Knob drag updates percentages live; percentages are also directly editable.

**Implementation scope tightened** — this issue ships **only** `plain`, `comparator`, and `iterator` (the surface area already in production). The other kinds (randomizer, weighted, distributor, multi-band comparator) get their own follow-on issue files opened at merge time. Designs stay in 233 for context but don't gate this issue's completion.

The dispatch table in 304 still lists all six kinds since it's a reference for the dispatch layer's eventual full surface area; 233 controls which of those land in phase 2.

Want to start implementing 233 now, or open the four follow-on issue files first so the design gets preserved as separate trackable tickets?
